No. 58. REPORT OF CONDITIONS IN JAPAN
DURING THE MONTH OF JULY, 1951.
The month of July this year for the most
part showed itselr to be unseasonable not only
in regard to atmospherio conditions, which through-
out most of the month seemed more like Maroh than
July, but also in regard to politioal affairs,
which refused to be submerged in the usual summer
calm. The events whioh caused most exoitement
Riots in
during the month under review were conneoted with
Korea
the serious riots which ocourred in Manohuria and
Korea between chinese and Koreans.*
The most serious riots ocourred in Heijo,
Korea, on July 5th, where it was reported that
approximately 72 Chinese were killed and ll8 in-
595
jured, many of whom later died. Many shops of
Chinese were damaged, and about forty polioemen
were injured. The direct cause of the outbreak
appears to have been retaliation for an attack
made by chinese upon Korean farmers at Wanpaoshan,
Manchuria, on July lst, when irrigation ditches
built by Koreans were destroyed by Chinese, ap-
parently with connivance of the Chinese police.
The disturbance in Korean oacurred at a most
unfortunate time, with the absence of the newly
Kaw-h.
appointed Governor General Ugak1, who had not yet
proceeded to his post. Muoh oritioism has been
direoted
* Embassy's Telegram No. 94, of July 8th, 1 p.m.;
Despatoh No. 29, of July 7, 1931, from Consul
General, Keijo; Despatch No. 30, of July 9, 19sl,
from Consul General, Keijo; Despateh No. 32, of
July 1l, 193l, from Consul General, Keijo.

=== Page 7 === (confidence 0.97)
- 8-
direoted against the Government Generel and mash
speculation has arisen as to the reasons for lack
of proper protection given the Chinese even after
the first minor riots had ooourred with warnings of
more serious ones to follow. The Japanese poliee
seemed from the first unable or unwilling to take
proper steps to prevent outrages committed by Koreans
upon Chinese. Although the police were admittedly
powerless, at no time was there any intention on
the part of the authorities to call out the troops
to aid them. One regiment was reported to have been
moved out of Heijo, the scene of the most serious riots,
to protect Japanese and Koreans in Manohuria against
retaliation by the chinese, although no suoh retali-
ations were reported to have occurred.
Although the disturbances in Korea and Manohuria
appear to have been merely race riots between the two
nationalities and interest in the riots seems to have
momentarily died down at the present time, it is feared
that the outbreak may have serious consequences, due
to its effect upon the whole Manohurian situation,
whioh is partioularly acute at the present time. The
Japanese Government has apparently realized, to some
extent, at least, the responsibility which it has in
the matter and has decided to lay aside Yen 800,000
out of the reserve fund for relief measures and to
provide ertra police protection.
Beports have appeared in the local press in-
Changes
dicating that the Japanese Government is contemplating
1n Man-
making radical changes in its polioy toward Manohurie.
churian
Although the reports hint at ohanges of the utmost
Poliex
importance in the Govermment's Manohurian poliey, it
has

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
has been impossible up to the present time to ob-
tain any information regarding the proposed change.
On July 7th began the second trial of forty
Comma-
leaders of the Japan Communist Party headed by Gaku
nist
Sano. The trial afforded an opportunity for the com-
Trial
munists to air their political views, so mmoh so that
the trial proceeded slowly with many interruptions,
as the judge was foroed to reprimand those on trial
for the language used. Before the start of the trial
the judge in charge of the proceedings stated that
complete freedom of speech could be indulged in and that
the publio would not be exoluded. As the trial pro-
ceeded, however, the controversial nature of the sub-
jects brought up caused the judge to attempt to ex-
ooB
clude the publio, thereby drawing vehement protests
4
from the communists. A group of communist laborers
8
attempted to give support to their fellows by sing-
ing communist songs before the opening of the court.
The singing took place in front of the imperial
crest whioh decorates the building, and it was re-
ported that this minor inoident might develop into
a serious problem due to the implied disrespeot to
the Imperial Family.
On July 5th the long contemplated merger of
Merger
proletarian parties in Japan took place with the
1.X
of Prole- formation of the Zenkoku Ronotaishuto, or All-Japan
tarian
Labor, Farm and Masses Party. Mr. Hisashi Aso,
Parties
Chief Seoretary of the Japan Masses Party, was
eleoted to the post of chief secretary of the newly
formed party.
The platform of the Zenkoku Bonotaishuto in-
eludes

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.98)
-4 -
oludes three basic prinoiples, an outline of whieh
is as follows:
1. That the party will fight for the pro-
tection and extension of the rights of oppressed
bodies of laborers, farmers, and proletarians.
2. The party will work for the reform of
the capitalist system.
3. The party will endeavor to enlarge and
strengthen all labor unions, unions of farmers,
and all other proletarian groups.
Policies were adopted covering politics, forein
policy of Japan, the present military system, judioi-
ary, labor, agrioultural communities, and social
affairs. The outstanding feature of the polioy re-
garding politios was the advocacy of a thorough re-
vision of the election law to permit the participa-
tion in elections of both men and women above eighteen
years of age, the abolition of the peace preservation
law of the polioe and of all other regulations tending
to the oppression of proletarians, the reform of the
central administration of the Government, and direot
eleotion of governors of prefectures, mayors of cities
and towns, and heads of villages.
In spite of the Government's efforts at allevi-
ation, it is reported that the unemployment situation
Unemploy- in Japan is growing more aoute. According to unem-
ment
ployment figures recently made publio by the Social
Bureau of the Home Ofrice, the total unemployment
for April of this year throughout Japan was 394,625,
and for May was 401,415. For the first time since
official figures became available in September, l929,
the total exoeeds 400,000. The unemployed in May
are classified as follows:
Salaried

A marked inorease was witnessed in Kanagase,
Hagasaki, Okayama, and Hiroshima, due to the dis-
missals of many naval workers in consequenoe of
the enforoement of the London Naval Treaty.
Muoh speculation has arisen over the arrival
Visit of in Japan on July 24th of Mr. Bugene Chen, Forein
Eugene
Minister of the unrecognized government whioh has
Chen
been established at Canton. Mr. Chen, mo is ao-
companied by his wife and suite, has endeavored to
avoid all publioity; the whole party has been
travelling under Japanese names. While nothing
definite is known as to Mr. Chen's real reason for
coming to Japan, it is reported in the Japanese
press that he desires to talk with Japanese leaders
44.
both in and out of office conoerning the polioy of
the Cantonese Government towards Japan. As the
Japanese Government considers Mr. Chen merely a
private Chinese citizen, it is reported that he has
been refused a formal interview with Baron Shidehara.
It has been learned, however, that informal meetings
between them have taken plaoe outside the Foreign
Office.
The Embassy was informed that in these meetings
there was no suggestion made by Chen that Japan
alter its polioy in regard to non-recognition of
separate governments in China or the shipment of arms.

REPORT OF CONDITIONS IN JAPAN
DURING THE LONTH OF AUGUST, 1931.
The month of ugust, ordinarily so quiet that
all ofricial rokyo abanaons the capital for the
mountains, this year was filled with activity
while all Japan turned air-minded in welcoming a
number or aistinguished forein aviators.
all Japan joined in welcoming colonel and rs.

The only cloud in the otherwise unbounded on-
thusiastic reception given the Lindberghs upon their

of te avy separtment viewed with suspioion the
8
fact that Colonel Lindbergh passed over and landed

lt.ou the of icials immeaiately denied the truth

No. 60
REPORT OF CONDITIONS IN JAPAN
DURING THE MONTH OF SEPTEMBER, 1931.
The month of September in Japan came in like a
lamb and went out like a lion. The newspapers of
Tokyo had so little to report during the first half of
the month that a good portion of space in the press
was given over to reporting the flights - and plights -
of various foreign aviators in Japan.
The latter
half of the month witnessed the development of a
critical situation in Manchuria which drew away public
attention from all other events.
Earthquake Anniversary:
September 1st marked the 8th anniversary of the
great earthquake, and the occasion was observed by memorial
services taroughout the Tokyo district. s though to
48
serve as a reminder of that awful event, there occurred
on the zlst an unusually strong earthquake in which
894.4
twelve persons were killed in the vicinity of Tokyo.
This earthquake, reported to be the most severe since
1924, caused, incidentally, some damage to the masonry
around the base of the building housing the Embassy.
Aviation:
The month of September opened with the Lindberghs
completing their program of entertainment in Tokyo.
They had been dined and feted until observers marveled

=== Page 30 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
at their endurance. The Lindberghs were enthusiastioally
and sincerely welcomed by the Japanese public, and every
courtesy was extended to them by officials and people.
There is little doubt that their visit demonstrated, and
assisted in strengthening, the feeling of good will
toward merica on the part of the Japanese. Colonel
Lindbergh was the recipient of an extraordinary honor
when, on September 10th, the Third Oräer of Merit of the
Cordon of the Rising Sun was conferred on him by the
Emperor.
As the Lindberghs were completins their official
schedule in Tokyo, the German aviatrix, Wiss Etzdorf,
arrived in Tokio on a solo flight from Europe and was
iven much publicity and entertainment.
The names of the merican aviators, Moyle and Allen,
and Herndon and Pangborn, were featured prominently in
the press auring September. oyle and Allen eventually
obtained permission for their projected flight, after
protracted ne otiations, and hopped off from Sabishiro
beach on September 8th, exactly one month from the time
of their arrival in Japan.
The application of Herndon and Pangborn remained
entangled in coils of red tape until the latter part of
the month, while officials debated whether or not a
permit should be granted to aviators who had broken
Japanese aviation laws. Permission was finally granted
to them in a communication from the Foreign Office
dated September 2lst.*
GOVERNMENT
*Embassy's despatch No. 334 of September 25,
1931.

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.96)
- 3 -
Government Readjustments:
▲ number of administrative changes have been
recommended by the Administrative and Financial Readjust-
ment Comnission, which has been working on a plan of
changes in line with financial retrenchment.
The
proposals put forward by the commission have been sub
mitted to the various departments of the government for
revision and amendment, and will be brought up for final
2
decision at a meeting of the Cabinet in Ootober.
0
number of the chan es recommended are regarded as certain
4
of accomplishment.
 Among these is the abolition of the
Department of Overseas ffairs, the newest of the
Ministries, which seems to have encountered much diffi-
culty in dischargine its supposed function of supervising
the colonial overmnents.  Board of Overseas Affairs
will be created to take over the funetions of the abol-
ished ministry, and will be attached directly to the
Cabinet and uncer the supervision of the Premier.
The Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry and the
Ministry of Commerce and Industry will be merged and re-
named the Winistry of Industry.
Merger and abolition
of various sections and Bureaus of several Departmonts
will be carried out.
The Minister of Finance, Mr.
Inouye, who is the moving figure behind all these
changes, hopes by them to effect a saving of Yen
100,000,000.
Resignation of Dr. Egi:
In connection with the proposal to abolish the
Lanh.
Ministry of Overseas Affairs, Mr. Hara, the Minister.
offered

=== Page 32 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
offered strenuous objection to the change.
 deadlook
ensued which was broken by the resignation on September
10th of Dr. Egi, Minister of Railways, on the ground of
ill health, and the appointment of Mr. Hara to replace
him. With Mr. Hara disposed of, the proposal to abolish
the Overseas Linistry met no effective opposition.
NorthernFisheries:
The old dispute between Japan and Soviet Russia
over fishery concessions in northern waters again came
up during September. The Embassy has reported that an
agreement had been reached whereby Japanese fishing in-
terests were to pay the rent for the concessions, and
other charges, at the rate of Yen O.325 to the ruble.
It appears now that the Soviet authorities are attempting
to interpret the agreement to mean that only rents on
the concessions are to be paid at the agreea rate, and
that certain other charges, including fines assessed by
the Soviets, are not payable at this rate. Negotiations
had not, at the end of the month, come to any satisfactory
conclusion.
Proletarian Day Disturbances:
More than 130 proletarian agitators were arrested
in Tokyo on September 6th, the International Proletarian
Youths' Day, and others were rounded up in Osaka.
number of attempts at demonstrations were made, but
were sternly suppressed by the police. Radioals of
various kinds were taken into custody in a number of
police raids made throughout the city.
Suspension

Fighting in Manchuria:
On September 18th, a section of railway tracks of
the South Manchuria Railway at Peitaying, near Mukden,
was blown up by a force of about 30o Chinese troops,
according to Japanese reports. Japanese railway guards,
who hurried to the scene of the explosion, came into
a4
conflict with the Japanese troops.
This incident fanned into flame tne feeling of
irritation which has been smouldering in the minds of
793.
Japanese over a long series of unsettled problems af-
fecting Japanese interests in Manchuria and China.
Anong these yuestions are the construction of railways
by the Cninese allegealy in contravention of existing
agreements, the problem of land rights, taxation,
Koreans in Manchuria, and the boycott of Japanese goods.
The most serious incident of this sort in the eyes of
the Japanese military was undoubtedly the killing by
Chinese soldiers of a Japanese staff officer, Captain
Nakamura, on June z7th.*
Following the skirmish at Peitaying, Japanese broops
occupied the city of Wukden and the arsenal, while,in
the north, the Japanese garrison seized Changohun.
Kirin
was apparently occupied on the zlst. Subsequently a
small Japanese force was sent on to Tunwha. On the west
Chienohatun
* Embassy's despatch No. 3z4 of September 12, 1931.

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
Chienchatun was occupied by a force sent out from
Szepinkai, and Japanese forces of observation were re-
ported as far as Taonanfu. Hsinmin has a small
Japanese force, while Antung and Yinkow were occupied.
The total Japanese force as originally given was
a little in excess of 10,c00, which was reinforced by
a mixea brigade of 4,U00 from Korea. The total
Japanese Torces outsiae of the Leasea Territory of
Kwantung are iven as something less than 15,000, or
approximately 16,000 for the whole of Manchuria, in-
cluding the garrison at Fort Arthur.
There seems to have been some friction between the
Army and other branches of the Governnent in this affair.
The Foreign office especially seemed to have received
rather meagre reports of what actually took place in
the regions where the army was operating.*
New Chinese inister:
ir. Chiang Tso-ping, the newly appointed Chinese
Linister to Japan, callea on Baron Shidehara at the
Foreign Office on September zind to announce his assumption
of office.
His arrival, and subsequent presentation of
701.93.
credentials, is pointed to as having some significance in
view of the critical state of Chinese-Japanese affairs.

REPORT OF CONDITIONS IN JAPAN
DURING THE MONTH OF OCTOBER. 1931.
I. POLITICAL RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
Nothing to report.
II. POLITICAL RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
Manchuria:
During Ootober the Japanese foroes continued in the
positions in Manchuria that they had occupied during the
latter part of September, with the exoeption of Tunhua,
from which troops were withdrawn on Ootober 5th. There
was no indication of intention to withdraw from other
places until the Chinese had agreed to the "basio prinoiples"
outlined in the statement of the Japanese Government
3
9
1ssued on Ootober 26, 1931. The movement of troops against
1
bands of Chinese bandits and ex-soldiers continued inter-
mittently while the prinoipal military action was the
bombing of Chinohow, where General Chang Hsueh-liang was
attempting to reorganize his government. The Japanese
insisted that the bombing was necessary because the Chinese
had fired on their planes while reconnoitering.
The civilian government continued, unable apparently
to control the polioy or acts of the military, while the
tenor of an important part of the publie became more
militant.* It was not unnatural that the publie should
* Despateh No. S85 of November 7, 1981.

=== Page 43 === (confidence 0.96)
一 8 -
have been so affected, for the military had directed
propaganda to this end, the Counoil of the League had,
with the exception of Japan's delegate, approved a
resolution regarded as detrimental to Japan's interests,
and certain aots of British offioials had convinoed the
country that Britain was using the League for selfish
ends against Japan.*
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL ACTIVITIES.
Profeotural Lleotions:
Bleotions carried out during October in all but a
few of the prefectures resulted in a deoided victory for
the Minseito, the Government party, whioh returned 808
representatives as against 635 for the Seiyukai, the
chief opposition party.**
The success of the Government party is not attributable
to its popularity. The Minseito has been, in fact, sub-
jected to wide-spread criticism, partioularly on aocount
of the conciliatory polioy of the government vis-a-vis
China. The publio, however, seems to be inolined to give
the present government sufficient opportunity to settle
the present situation in Manohuria. The publio is still
reluotant to trust the Seiyukai with the reins of Govern-
ment at a time of national emergeney, remembering the un-
savory administration of that party's sovernment which
preceded the present Minseito government.
The proletarian parties, from whom so mmch has been
hoped, sustained a heavy loss in these eleetions.

Triel of Hamaguchi's assailent:
On November Znd began the trial of Tomeo Sagoye, whe
attempted to assassinate Premier Hamaguch on November 14,
1930, in Tokyo Station.* The original charge against
Sagoya was that of attempted murder but as a result of the
subsequent death of the ex-Premier, the charge was changed
to that of murder. Being tried with Sagoya is Yoshikatsu
Matsuki, who is accused of having aided the would-be
assassin. Due to the change of oharge from attempted
murder to actual murder the defense has asked and obtained
a postponement of the trial from November 6th to November
18th, the extra time being needed to procure evidence to
refute the charge that ir. Hamiguchi's death was caused
by the bullet of the assailant.
Military Power and Autumn Manoeuvres:
▲ further indication of the power of the Military
clique in opposition of the Government appears in the
controversy regarding the attendance of the Emperor at
the autumn manoeuvres in Kyushu. The Government contends
that it will be a more impressive demonstration to the
world of its determination in its attitude toward the
situation in Manchuria if the Kmperor feels it of sufficient
importance to canoel his trip to Kyushu in order to be
present in Tokyo when the League meets on November l6th.
The Military olaim that it will be more impressive if the
meeting of the League is not considered of sufficient im-
portance to cause the Imperor to change his plans, As usual
the army has won and the Emperor will not cancel his plams
to attend the Manoeuvres.

V. GOVERNMENT FINANCES.
Raising of the Discount Rate:
On Ootober 6th the Bank of Japan announced the rise
of its offioial discount rate the new rate going into
effect the previous day.
The new rates are as follows:
1. Discount rate for commeroial bills, - l6 rin per
diem.
2. Interest rate on loans on the security of national
bonds, over 17 rin per diem.
894.576
3. Interest rate on loans on the security or negoti-
able papers other than national bonds, - over 18 rin per
diem.
4. Interest rate for overdraft account and for over-
drafts by correspondence, - over 20 rin per diem.
Lir. Hijikata, Governor of the Bank of Japan is quoted
as declaring that the present raise in disoount rate is in-
tended to cheok the outflow of gold. There has been much
critioism among banking and business men of the aotion of
the Bank of Japan, but both Mr. Hijikata, Governor of the
Bank, and Mr. Inouye, inister of Finance declare that
there will be no bad effect on finanoial oiroles.
On October 5th a further increase of two rin a day per
one hundred yen was announoed, the Minister of Finanoe
stating that although the present rise is admitted to be
too high and may cause much hardship to business men, it
is necessary for several reasons, the ohief among them
being the determination of the Government to maintain the
Gold Standard.
The Gold Embarse:
Much speculation has arisen lately ameng fimemeiel

the Bank of Japan to prevent the export of gold is the
raising of its discount rate twice in one month as men-

The Yokohama Speoie Bank hitherto has met all demands
for dollar exchange even though it has reason to believe

against bills for acceptance. Small remittances will also
be undertaken but the bank will refuse the remittance of
large sums. Thus it would appear that a practical if not am
official gold embargo is in existence at the present time.

VI. GENERAL FINANCIAL AND ECONOMIC CONDITIONS.
Plight of Shipowmers.
Doubly hit by the Chinese boyoott and the British
Gold Embargo, Japan's shipping trade is reported to be in
a serious plight. Nearly 1,500,000 tons are at present
serving in Europe and elsewhere but owing to the fall in
sterling over 30 per cent, it is believed, will be foroed
to withdraw. Many companies are attempting to place
894
their excess tonnage on coastal service, but chartesers
are so difficult to find that most companies will be foroed
to tie up a large part of their ships. Purther, the
Nippon Yusen Kaisha and other shipping companies are un-
able to dispose of their superannuated vessels for break-
ing up and similar purposes because British ships are
offered cheaper, thanks to the depreciated sterling.
Many ship-owners are greatly worried over the situation
and have even suggested a cooperative tying up, though
this plan has not met with any greet amount of apprevel.
Many ahipowners in Kobe and Osake have demended an te

X..MISCELLANEOUS.
The Flight of Pangborn and Herndon:
On October 5th, after a forced residence in Japan of
just two months, and after causing international complioa-
tions by their unintentional violation of Japanese flying
regulations, the aviators, Pangborn and Herndon took off
from the beaoh at Sabushiro,their announced destination
being Salt Lake City, over 5, Ooo miles away. To add to
the long series of misfortunes enoountered by the aviators
in Japan their departure was somewhat delayed by the dis-
appearance of their maps, whioh were evidently removed
from the plane without their knowledge. . In spite of the
great opposition to their flight raised by Japanese
officialdom from the moment of their landing in Japan,
the statements issued by High Government officials in-
oluding Premier Wakatsuki, Foreign Minister Baren Shidehare,
the Minister of Communications, and many others were
unanimous in praising the flyers for the eccomplishment
of their remarkable feat.

President of the South Manekuria Railwey.

=== Page 58 === (confidence 0.98)
No. 61
REPORT OF CONDITIONS IN JAPAN
DURING THE MONTH OF NOVEMBER 1931.
Hardly a day passed in November in which the jingle
of bells of gogai ( extra) newsboys was not heard. The
one matter that has occupied the attention of the whole
Japanese nation has been the Manchurian situation.
Lesser news has been crowded out of public mind and
out of the press by the all-absorbing news from Manohu-
ria. Even an important domestic political manoeuvre,
which ordinarily would have caused wide sensation, re-
ceived comparatively small attention in the press.
I.
POLITICAL RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
Nothing to report.
II.
POLITICAL RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
Military Movements in Manohuria.
The first of November found the Japanese in control
of all the points occupied during the first outbreak of
793.94
hostilities in Manchuria, except Tunhua, and with Chinese
opposition concentrated at two points. In the north,
the Heilungkiang Army, headed by General Ma Chan Shan
loomed as a menace to the Japanese, and in the south,
near Chinohow, the foroes remaining loyal to Marshal
Chang Hsueh Liang were concentrating.
General Ma had concentrated approximately 20,000
trooge

=== Page 59 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
troops, inoluding the remmants of the Kirin Amy. in
vioinity of Tsitsihar, and encouraged, the Japaneme be
lieved, by Marshal Chang, was preparing for an attack
on the Japanese. He had refused to consider the Japanese
demand to repair the Taonan-Anganchi railway bridge over
the Nonni River, destroyed by his own troops, whioh the
Japanese deemed essential to the proteotion of a property
financed by Japanese funds. Japanese engineers were sent
to repair the bridge, and an advance guard was posted to
protect the bridge. This guard was attacked by Ma's
troops on the Srd, and an engagement resulted in which
there were severe fighting and heavy oasualties on
both sides. The Japanese, witn the aid of reinforce-
ments, succeeded in putting the Chinese to flight, and
the routed army retired in disorder toward their base
at Tsitsihar, on the 5th and 6th. Thereafter, for a
few days, the situation remained comparatively quiet,
except for occasional skirmishes, during which time
the Japanese brought up reinforcements to make a total
of approximately 2,000 troops, mostly of the 2nd divi-
sion, stationed around Tahsing.
Overtures were made by the Japanese looking to a
settlement of the local situation, but were refused by
General Ma. Whereupon the Japanese attaeked, and with
airplane and armored motor cars engaged the Chinese at
a point north of Tahsing. This was on the 17th. By
the following day the Japanese had swept through the
Chinese defence, seized Anganchi, crossed the Chimese
Eastem Railway and ocoupied Tsitsihar, putting the

=== Page 60 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Heilungkiang army to complete route.
With Mukden, Kirin and Tsitsihar under oontrol,
only one provincial capital in Manohuria, Chengtieh
(Jehol) just north of the Great Wall, remained indepen-
dent of the Japanese. The defeated Heilungkiang army
scattered in all direotions, while General Ma fled to
Hai lung with a remnant of his army. The Japanese left a
small contingent of Japanese troops on guard in Tsitsihar,
and moved the main force toward Mukden in antioipation
of hostilities in the south.
In the meantime on the southern "front", the
Chinese troops loyal to -arshal Chang were being re-
inforoed by men and equipment. The activity of the troops
in this area caused the Japanese to move their forces
southward from Mukden, and on November 24 and the'days
succeeding the Japanese advanced toward Chinohow. The
Japanese military leaders stated at the time that this ad-
vance was only for the purpose of driving out bandits, but
admitted that an attack on Chinohow " might become
necessary". The Japanese representative at the League
Council at Paris had formally declared that the Japanese
army would not attack Chinchow, but in spite of this
assurance the steady advance of Japanese troops aroused
the gravest apprehension.
Additional troops were sent from Mukden, and an
advance began from Hsinmin to Paichipao, where fighting
was enoountered with Chinese troops. From there the
advance continued to Takushan and then to Koupantse,
35 miles from Chinohow. At Koupantse the advanee was
halted by orders from Tokyo, and on November 28 the
troops began to withdraw to eastward of the Liao river,
neas

=== Page 61 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
near Hsinmin, whence the advance had began. Jape
military leaders deny that the withdrawal of troops
from the Chinchow region had any relation to the
statement of the American Secretary of state, but the
consensus of Japanese opinion is to the contrary. It
must, however, be borne in mind that Chang had approxi-
mately 100,000 troops near Chinchow and 30,000 well
armed troops in that city, a foroe which would have proved
no easy opponent for the small Japanese contingent.
The latest news from Manchuria indicates that there
is likelihood of further disturbances in the Tsitsihar
region, where a small contingent of Japanese troops was
left to guard the city. Some 3,Ooo Heilungkiang troopa
are reported to be preparing to advance on Tsitsihar.
The Ja anese troops are. reported to be suffering
greatly from the unaccustomed cold of Manchuria. Frost
bite has crippled a number of men.
This, in bare outline, is the sequence of military
activities in Manchuria. One or two details must be
added to complete the picture . Mention must be made of
Soviet Russia in this connection.
Rumors of Soviet Interference.
Constant rumor conneots the Soviets with the
Manchurian situation. The Japanese press and certain
military offioials have openly accused the Soviets of
711.93
giving aid to the Chinese. Chinese war lords are re-
ported to boast that they fear nothing from Japan as
Soviet Russia stands behind them. As far as reliable
evidence shows, the Soviets have, however, maimteimed

=== Page 62 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
an attitude of neutrality to an extent that is some-
what surprising, in view of the importance of their
interests in the regions affeoted by hostilities.
Molotov, chairman of the Counoil of Peoples Commisars, is
reported to have said in a speech " We are watohing the
situation in Manchuria carefully and our polioy is not to
interfere in their(Chinese and Japanese) affairs and not
to permit them to interfere in our affairs". It would
seem that this policy has been fairly well carried out
in spite of rumors to.the contrary. The Soviet Govern-
ment has evinced some uneasiness over Japanese aotivi-
ties in the region of Heilungkiang Province, partioularly
around Anganchi and the Chinese astern Railway, but it
has disavowed any intention of taking part in the dis-
pute, and resents statements t) that effeot made by
Japanese officials. Notes in this connection have been
exchanged between the Soviet and Japanese Governments.
The "Boy Emperor".
Mr. Henry Pu-yi, former "boy emperor" of China has
been reported as staying in Mukden. According to rumor
893.001
the Japanese are holding him in reserve with a view to
setting him up as the head of an autonomous Manohurian
goverrment.
III. INTERNAL POLITICAL ACTIVITIES.
Cabinet Change Rumored.
A persistent rumor of an impending change in the
Cabinet has embarrassed the Government.
See Eabassy's despatch No. s06 of Movember n0.

=== Page 63 === (confidence 0.98)
-8-
was started a month ago after the Premier made a meries
of calls on various persons in high authority. such
calls often presage a political change in Japan. Mr.
Adachi, the Home Minister and a powerrul politioian,
caused a sensation, in the early part of the month, when,
on departing for a trip away from the capital, he informed
press representatives that he would weloome a national
or coalition cabinet. Apparently he had not consulted
with the other members of the cabinet nor with his
party, the Minseito, in this regard, for his remarks
caused a veritable panio in these organizations. The
public read his statement to mean that either he had lost
confidence in the cabinet of which he is a member, or that
a schism exists which will cripple the oabinet. This
remark, coming on top of the real difficulties of the
cabinet, namely: the opposition to the Shidehara
diplomacy, and the dissatisfaction with the finanoial
policies of Mr. Inouye, the Finance Minister, oause
many observers to conclude that a cabinet ohange is
impending.
The real reason back of Mr. Adachi's move is hard
to identify. It is rumored that Baron Wakatsuki, the
Premier, first suggested the idea of a coalition cabinet
ead.
to Mr. Adaohi, but withdrew his support because of op-
position from Mr. Inouye. It is certain that Mr. Adachi
has long cherished an ambition to be premier, and it
may be that he hopes to create a political melee from
which he would emerge, as key man of his party, with the
premier's portfolio.
The Seiyukai, the opposition party, are plesssd wit
the turn of events, bat its leadere have de

they stand to gain by the embarrassment of the Gove
party.
IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION.

V.
GOVERNMENT FINANCES.
The Budget.
994.51
The preparation of the budget for the next fisoal
year has been giving no little difficulty to the Finance
Minister, Mr. Inouye. At a meeting of the cabinet on
October 31 and at subsequent meetings of the Minseito
leaders, it was decided that the pronouneed no-loan polioy
of the present Government must be abandoned, and that the
budget deficit be made up by enterprise and by increased
taxation.
These measures are necessary because of the marked
decline in revenue, calculated to exoeed Yen 140,000,000
in the next fiscal year; this in spite of heroio measures
taken in the direction of economy and administrative
reform. The aggregate expenditure is fixed at about
Yen 1,480,000,000, whereas revenue is reokoned to be
approximately Yen 1,300,000,000. Even if all the
recommendations of the Finance Minister are carried
out, there will still be a defioit of over Yon 30,000,000,
and acceptance of theze recommendations in full is quite
unlikely. The proposed tax inorease plan provides for
increases in the income tax, death duties, gasolene tax

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
and inoreases of oustoms duties, notably on wheet, pie
iron and steel, by whioh it is hoped to raise appro-
ximately Yen 50,000,000. The remainder will be made
up in enterprise bonds, notably for telephone, road
works and earthquake issues. The Finance Ministry may
compelled to issue a loan for the specifio purpose of
making up the gap between revenue and e xpenditure. It
is said that war bonds will be issued in conneotion with
expenditures on the army in Manchuria. There is some
speculation in the press as to whether the market, with
its fast dwindling funds, will be able to absorb all these
loans. The announcement of the budget for the next year
has already been delayed by about three weeks from the
usual time. It is expected that the final estimates will
be ready for presentation within a few days.
Export of Gold Specie.
During November there was widespread interest in the
94.5151
possibility of a reimposition of an embargo on the export
from Japan of gold specie and agitation in favor of suoh a
step increased. On November loth the Diet members of the
Opposition Party met and passed a resolution advocating
an immediate prohibition of free export of gold; oertain
newspapers, the Tokyo JIJI in partioular, published
editorials denouncing the Government's adherence to the
polioy of free export; Mr. Sanji Muto's book, whioh is
strongly in favor of a gold embargo, entered the olass of
"best seller", 600,000 copies having been reperted sold
during a period of less than six weeks; a number or
members of the Tokyo Chamber of Commeree announeed
themselves in favor of an embargo.
Several feetors affeeting

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
such a step. The gold reserve had decreased by November
30th to Yen 542,716,000, about half of what it was when
the embargo was removed on January 11, 1930. The Japa-
nese military action in lManohuria continued to be a drain
on the Government's financial resources, while the re-
sultant intensified Chinese boycott and interruption of
communications in Manchuria adversely affeoted Japanese
foreign trade which was already depressed. Japan's
foreign trade continued to suffer also from the decline
in the value of sterling. The delay of the Finance
Minister in balancing the budget added to the widespread
feeling that some stringent financial measure might be
necessary.
These various factors, however, did not seem to
affect the Finance Minister in his polioy of free export
of gold specie. On November 25th, for example, he made
a speech at Osaka in which he showed no inclination to
return to the gold embargo, explaining that no signi-
ficant outflow of gold would be required in settlement
of the adverse balance of trade.
VI. GENERAL FINANCIAL AND ECONOMIC CONDITIONS.
Rice Crop.
The official forecast of the l93l rice crop places
the total yield at 55,027,910 koku. This is a deoline
994.61317
of nearly 12,000,000 koku from last year, and 1s the
smallest yield since l9l4. Adverse weather conditions
and inseot depredations are said to account for the poor
orop.
Trade

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
Trade with China.
The hostilities between the Japanese and Chimes
troops in Tientsin have dealt & severe blow to Japam's
export trade with North China, where the anti-Japane
boycott movement was less severe than in Central China.
Tientsin, until t he outbreak of hostilities, was the
693.94
largest consuming center of Japanese cotton goods and
miscellaneous articles in China, excepting Manohuria.
Commercial transactions have almost entirely been
stopped. Japanese ships now sailing there carry prac-
tically no cargo. Six large spinning mills in Tientsin
owned by Japanese, which formerly produoed one third
of the total production of North China, have either
suspended operations or have curtailed production.
Reports indicate that Japanese mills in Shanghai are
producing between 30 and 40 percent of normal, but are
forced to seek markets in other countries on account of
the boycott. Competition from British cottons, because
of the depreciation of the pound, is giving further
anxiety to Japanese exporters. Other Japanese factories
and merohants in Tientsin and Shanghai are faoing bank-
ruptoy. Out of 40 Japanese factories in Shanghai, of
any consequence, 33, it is stated, have closed their
doors since last month. Many have sent their Japanese
employees baok to Japan.
British Tariff.
A further antieipated blow to Japanese experters is
the new British proteotive tariff, whieh probably will
affect Japanese exports of hosiery, silk textiles,
brashes, toye and hats .

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
Gold Shipments.
On December 1 the Yokohama Specie Bank shipped
its seventeenth consignment of gold to the United
States since October 3, following the suspension
of British gold payments. This is believed to be the
last specie movement for the time being, although
further shipments vill probably have to be made in the
near uture. Total of gold shipped since Ootober 3
amounts to Yen 296,500,000. The specie reserve of
the Bank of Japan vhich, on Uctober 3, was 818, 366, 000,
has declined to approximately 550,000,000. The specie
reserve ratio on November 28 was 40.3% .
inticipating a renewal of the gold embargo in Japan,
speculators have been buying heavily in dollars. To
prevent the drain of gold, the Government countered
by raising the discount rate twice since October 3,
and has threatened to raise it again. Financial circles
favor the defence of the yen, but industrial leaders
are hard hit by the resulting tightness of money.
Speculators vere caught short by the Government's move
which raised the yen to a slight premium over dollars.
There was a wave of resentment stirred up by
these specula tive purchases to dollars. The Mitsui
Bank, which handled many of these purchases, was the
scene of a demonstration against the "prostitution of
the yen". Curiously enough, the press is almost
unanimous in opposing the Government's finanoial
moves. A few months ago the press took a united
stand against the replacement of the gold embargo,
whereas now the editorial writers condemn Minister
Inouye for standing in the way of the natural course

=== Page 69 === (confidence 0.98)
-12-
of consequences, and are advocating the replacement
of the gold ban.
Japanese Shipping.
Japanese shippers estimate that the Manchurian
894.85
incident has cost them Yen 55,820,000. Approximately
10% of Japunese ships re tied up, the figure for
unemployed bottoms being 414,120 tons, as compared
with 290,000 tons in mid-0ctober.

Outbreak in Formosa by Aborigines.
Four Japanese police officers, two women and one
child, were reported killed. in a raid by aborigines of the
Kayo tribe on a police post at Pistan, Formosa, on November
7th. The Kayo tribe resides near the Musha tribe, which
started the trouble last year in which several Japanese
were killed. No reason for the outbreak has been given by
the officials concerned.

I. POLITICAL RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
L. Associated Press Report of the Seeretary's
Remarks.
Any resentment direoted toward the Seeretary as a
result of the garbled Associated Press report of his
remarks to the press on November 87th regarding the
situation about Chinchow seemed to disappear som-
pletely early in the month under review. Domestie
repereussions are discussed under Section III of
this report.
B. The Secretary's Memorandum of December 2and.
The transmission toward the close of the month
by the American, British, and Freneh Ambassadors of
memoranda from their Governments to the Japanese
Foreign Offiee in respeet to the menacing situation
in the vieinity of Chinchew resulted in me develop-
ments of impertanee. The statement of the Japamese
Government and the memerandum of the Foreig effiee,
prepared in reply to the Seeretary's memerandum, wese
treasmitted to the Department in the mabassg's despete

=== Page 79 === (confidence 0.96)
- 8 -
Japanese peeple as the most important of the threes
probably because the attitude of the United states
since the outbreak of the Manohariam affair has
been, in Japanese opinion, the most impertant cut-
side factor. At any rate, press somment dealt primsi-
pally with the Secretary's memorandum. As reperted
in the above-mentioned despatch, such somment was
surprisingly free from bitterness, the strongest
terms used being "unwarranted", "ineonsistent", and
"premature". It seemed that the publie, as their
opinion is refleoted in the press, regarded the
Japanese position in respeot to Chinohow as so
beyond reproach that the counsel of foreign coun-
tries, including that of the United States, was
viemed as a supererogation founded on misinform-
ation and misconception.

=== Page 96 === (confidence 0.98)
NO. 64.
REPORT OF CONDITIONS IN JAPAN
DURING THE MONTH OF JANUARY, 1932.
I.
POLITICAL RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
A. Exohange of Notes.
On the occasion of the occupation of Chinchow, the
Embassy addressed a note to the Minister for Foreign
Affairs dated January 8, stating that the American Govern-
ment could not admit the legality of any situation de facto
in Manchuria nor would it recognize any treaty which
might impair treaty rights of the United States in that
region. This note was not well received in Japan, partly
on account of an unfortunate error made in the press
translation.* There was a feeling in certain quarters
that the United States was interfering unreasonably.
The reply from the Japanese Government was received
on January 16,** which, in somewhat vague language, re-
plied that application of the treaties referred to in
the Embassy's note must be made with reference to the
state of affairs as they exist, but gave assurance that
"Japan entertains no territorial aims or ambitions in
Manchuria".
193.94/3739
* See Embassy's despatoh No. 4?
of Janua 15, 1932.
993.9473734
** For tert see Embassy's despatoh No: 4o1/or Jammary 16,
1932.

=== Page 97 === (confidence 0.99)
B.
Chamberlain Affair.
The incident in which United States Consul
Culver B. Chamberlain was assaulted at Mukden by a
Japanese interpreter and two soldiers, occurred on
January 3rd.
II. POLITICAL RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES.
A.
Occupation of Chinohow.
The long expected occupation of Chinchow by
Japanese forces took place on January 3. Japanese
troops of the 20th Division,numbering approximately
5,Ooo,under Lieutenant General Muro peacefully en-
tered the city from which the Chinese forces of
General Chang Hsueh -liang had withdrawn without
resistance, southward of the Great Wall. The advance
of the Japanese on this occasion, as compared with
their campaign of November* was made slowly, evidently
giving the Chinese troops ample opportunity to evacuate
their position without fighting.
With Chinchow in their hands, the Japanese con-
sidered that their campaign for the ocoupation of South
Manchuria had reached its objective and that major
military operations had come to an end. The first
major action had been against General Ma in the north**,
and with that successfully oompleted, there had remained
opposed
693,00P.R.T46
* See Embassy's despatoh No. 404
of December 4, 1931.
** Ibid.

=== Page 98 === (confidence 0.99)
opposed to them in Manchuria only the Chinese troops
in Chinchow, besides, of course, numerous bands of
"bandits". The Harbin situation which later re-
quired action by Japanese troops, had not arisen.
B.
Anti-Bandit Campaign.
With the occupation of Chinchow all organized
resistance to Japanese authority was driven beyond the
borders of Manchuria. Throughout the month of January,
however, there have been constant reports of clashes
between the Japanese troops and bandits. Some of the
latter are the " hunghutzes", long the scourge of
Manchuria, while others are renmants of the defeated
Chinese forces and practically indistinguishable from
the older hands of outlaws.
The majority of these clashes have occurred within
a radius of a hundred miles of lMukden, in regions where
Japanese garrisons are posted. Outside of this dis-
trict there are, no doubt, numberless bands of Chinese
irregular soldiers and bandits who carry on depreda-
tions with little molestation. The Japanese invaders
have no litile problem before them in subduing these
roving marauders.
The most serious of these engagements was at Chinhsi,
west of Chinchow, where on the loth of January a cavalry
regiment under Lieutenant Colonel Koga encountered a
superior force of Chinese and was nearly wiped out, the
commanding officer himself being killed.
C.The Harbin Campaign.
On Jamuary 13, General Hsi Chia, Chairman of the
new Kirin provinoial government and understood to be a
Japanese

=== Page 99 === (confidence 0.99)
Japanese tool, forwarded a demand to General Ting
Chao, head of the Pinhsien Government near Harbin
and commander of the Chinese Eastern Railway Guards,
that he surrender in accordance with the proposed plan
to unify the Three Eastern Provinces and to establish
an autonomous government. This, General Ting refused
to do. Thereupon General Hsi despatched troops toward
Hurbin which clashed with General Ting's forces on
January 27th. General Hsi's troops were repulsed,
and following the enga ement soldiers of various kinds
began lootin in Harbin. In order to protect Japanese
nationals in Harbin the Japanese despatched troops
from Changchun over the southern section of the Chinese
Eastern Railway.
parently the Japunese troops encountered un-
expected rusistance from General Ting's troops. General
Tamon with a proximately 5,500 Japunese troops was not
able to make an entry to Harbin until February 5, nearly
a week after leaving Jhangc un. General Ting and most
of his troops escaped to the northward.
D.
I.ianchurian Independence.
Press reports and rumor continue to refer to the
impending establishment of an independent government in
Manchuria. While no official statements have been issued
and no definite information is forthcoming, it is under-
stood that the Chinese authorities of Kirin, Heilunkiang,
Mukden and Jehol have been oarrying on negotiations, with
Japanese encouragement, looking forward to the establish-
ment of a government independent of Nanking. The soheme
most

=== Page 100 === (confidence 0.98)
most frequently bruited in the press is that of an
"independent Manohurian Government", by whioh is
meant, evidently, an autonomous government with
Japanese advisers and with Japanese support.
The Japanese authorities are apparently by no
means united in their plans for the future of Manohuria.
Various views have been expressed by the military au-
thorities as to the best course to be pursued when
order is established. So fur the Government at Tokyo
has done nothing of a concrete nature in this direction
beyond sending a committee of civilians to Mukden to
confer with the military authorities as to plans for the
future. The results of this conference have not yet
been made public.
The Japanese would no doubt desire to witness the
establishment of a regime something on the order of that
of Chang Tso Lin before his association with the Nanking
authorities. With such a government they could negotiate
without reference to Nanking and could further their
own interests while maintaining the fiction of an in-
dependent Manchuria..
According to the looal press the Chinese Government
has submitted a warning to Tokyo that any agreement or
pledge exchanged between the Japanese Government and the
new Chinese administration in Manchuria will not be
reoognized by the Central Government of China.
B. The Shanghai Affair.
Feeling in Shanghai between the Japanese community
and the Chinese, already tense because of the hostilities
in Manchuria, had been intensiried by disparaging statementa
r1

=== Page 101 === (confidence 0.98)
6
regarding the Japanese Emperor made by a Chinese
newspaper. On January l8th a party of Japanese
priests was assaulted by a Chinese mob on the streets
of Shanghai. The Japanese Consul General presented a
strong ncte of protest to the Chinese mayor of Shanghai
demanding punishment for the assailants, an apology from
the Chinese authorities, and disbandment of anti-Japanese
societies and measures to protect Japanese subjeots.
Meanwhile resentment for this assault resulted in
a number of retaliatory attacks by bands of Japanese
bravos on Chines persons and property. Following a
failure to receive what he considered a satisfactory
reply to the Japanese demands, the Japanese Consul General
in Shanghai served an ultimatum to the Mayor on January
27th. Japanese naval forces were moved to Shanghai and
had taken a threatening attitude. A clash between armed
forces of Japanese and Chinese occurred on January 29th.
This, in bare outline, is the course of events leading
up to Sino-Japanese hostilities in Shanghai.
F.Proposed Non-Aggression Paot With Soviet Russia.
The report of a proposal of a non-aggression paot
by the Soviet Government Government caused a stir of interest
in Tokyo*. Mr. Yoshizawa was approaohed at Mosoow en route
from Paris to Japan by the Soviet Foreign Commissar with a
proposal for such a treaty, while in Tokyo the Soviet
Ambassador took up the proposal with the Japanese Foreign
Offioe.
The
761.171/7
Vide Embassy's despatoh No. 476 or Jamuary 30, 1932.

=== Page 102 === (confidence 0.99)
The Japanese Government has given no direet
answer to the proposals, but it is understood that it
has no intention of concluding any such treaty at
present. The official attitude seems to be that a
treaty of the kind proposed is unnecessary in view of
the existence of the Kellogg Treaty, and that moreover
Japan does not care to limit further her freedom of
action in view of possible conflicts of interests between
Japan and Russia.
III.
INTERNAL POLITICAL ACTIVITIES.
A. Attempt Against the Emperor*.
On January 8, as the Emperor and his attendants
were returning to the Palace from the Grand Military
Manoeuvres, a Korean by the name of Li Ho-Sho threw an
explosive missile at the procession, which damaged the
carriage in which the Minister of the Household Depart-
ment was riding. The Emperor himself was not endangered.
The offender was immediately captured and no information
regarding his probable fate has been made public.
Because of the extraordinary reverence with which
the Emperor is regurded, this attack caused a tremendous
shock to the country. In accordance with the Japanese
ideas of responsibility, the new Inukai Cabinet resigned
en bloc, together with high police officials. Subsequently.
the Emperor commanded the Cabinet to remain in office.
The Cabinet met and agreed to follow this command. For
doing so the Cabinet has incurred the hostility of many
894.001H61/59
Japamse
* Embassy's despatoh No. 460/or January 16? 1932.

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.99)
Japanese who regard any action against the Emperor
as necessitating retirement of the Cabinet, and who
point out as a precedent the resignation of the
Yamamoto Cabinet in 1924 following a similar attempt
 the then Prince Regent. This inoident has very
materially affected the fortunes of the Seiyukai, the
Government Party, which may expect bitter attaoks in
the Diet for remainine in ofrice, and which will
certainly lose many votes in the impending general
elections, in consecuence of its decision.
B. Conference of Prefectural Governors.
The annual Conference of Prefectural Governors
met at Tokyo on January 14 and 15. This conference is
chiefly of historical interest, as it was originally
designed to fill the rol2 called for in the Meiji pro-
clamation Whereby " all measures shall be decided by
public discussion". The demand for a more representative
and elective body eventually caused the formation of
the Imperial Diet, leavin the"Conference of Prefectural
Governors" no function beyond assembling to hear a report
upon governmental policy delivered by the Government
leaders and to listen to a homily upon the duties and
conduct of governors. The conference is still opened
by the Emperor, whose presence bears witness to the
importance with whicn the Conference was invested at
one time.
On the occasion of this meeting the Premier made
one state ment of interest in his address: " In regard
to Manohuria, the present Government not only respeots
the

=== Page 104 === (confidence 0.99)
9
the existing international treaties, but intends to
make the Manchurian region one .of pe ace and security
for all peoples, observing the polioy of the Open Door,
by bringin to an end the threat to life and property
which has existed heretofore, due to the continual
state of war prevailing in China."
C. Dissolution of the Diet.
As reported by the Embassy*, the Imperial Diet
in its 6oth session was dissolved on the day of its
convening after the holiday recess. The dissolution
had been enerally anticipated and caused little surprise.
The Gover ment (Seiyukai) explained this step by stating
that it could not carry out its policies in the face of
the large opposition majority existing in the lower
house.
The General Election has been set for February 2oth.
The Goveriment has been busy preparing for the election
by replacing prefectural governors and police officials
appointed during the Minseito resime, and has been
building up an election machine throughout the country.
The Seiyukai, being the Government Party, is con-
ceded the better chance of success in the elections.
It has the advantage of good organization and able
leadership. It has, moreover, cooperated more closely
with the military authorities in their ope rations in
China than the Minseito, and oan e xpeot support from
them.

Financial Measures.
On Jamuary 23 the Finance Minister submitted
t the Throne drafts of two Impe rial ordinances. The first

funds covering national bonds. These me asures will
be referred to the Privy Council before receiving
Imperial sanction. This procedure has been necessitated
by the dissolution of the Diet and the consequent failure
of action on the new budget for 1932-1933.
VI.
GENERAL FINANCIAL AND ECONOMIC CONDITIONS.
A. Government forbids silk experts prpoeeding to Russia.
The press reports that six Japanese silk experts who
had contracted to assist Soviet sericultural industry,
were forced to abrogate their contraots by the Japanese
Government. It is stated that the Japanese Government
feared the loss of technical seorets of the Japanese
sericultural industry.
B. Gold Shipments.
On January 30th, a total of Yen 89,000,000 in
gold had been shipped abroad since the imposition of
the gold embargo on December l3th. It is antioipated
that no further shipments of gold will be made in the

near future as this sum, together with certain gold

C. Rice.
The Department of Agriculture and Forestry an-
nounced on January 2lst that the 1931 rice crop amounted
to 55,215,263 koku, a decrease of 17 per cent. from the
previous year and 9 per cent. from the last five year
average. The Seiyukai Party is reported as favoring
a government monopoly of rice.
D. Fisheries.
It is re orted that the Mitsui interests have been
able to gain control of the Nichiro Fishery Company by
political manoueuvres following the downfall of the former
Cabinet. By gaining control they have worsted the Mitsu-
bishi interests which bave had the sales right for the
products of the Nichiro Company for some years.
E. General.
Since the imposition of the gold ban, domestic con-
ditions have shown some improvement. Prices have risen to
some extent and the wage index for the principal oities
of Japan shows a moderate increase. Shipping has received
a stimulus as Japanese vessels can now compete more easily
with those of other countries. Exchange quotations versus
the dollar have remained between 35 cents and 38 cents
during the month of January.

I.
POLITICAL RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES:
Exchange of Notes.
On February 2nd, in accordance with Department'
instruotion, the Embassy forwarded a note to the Foreign
Office, submitting four proposals for cessation of acts of
violence in China as follows: cessation of warlike pre-
parations; withdrawal of combatants from mutual contaot;
the establishment of neutral zones to separate the com-
batants; and negotiations to be made to settle all out-
standing controversies between the two nations.
A reply was received from the Japanese Govern-
ment on February 4(see Embassy's telegram No. 39 of
February 4, 10 P.M.), requiring that the Chinese first
cease their threatening activities; refusing to cease
preparations for hostilities; stating that the Japanese
Government " has no objection to its Consul and Commander
entering into negotiations" for the separation of the two
forces and, in case of necessity, for the establishment
of a neutral zone; and refusing to consider negotiations
to settle " all outstanding controversies" if Manohuria
was to be inoluded in the scope of the diseussion.
On February 26th the Embassy addressed a memo-
randum to the Foreign Ofrice requesting the Japanese
Government to move warships in Shanghai to a pesitiom
less liable to endanger the Settlement by fire drem fm
the Chinese troops, and requesting that in futuse

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
Japanese troops be landed at such points as would
endanger the Settlement.
A reply was received from the Pereign ofries
on February 29th stating that the message had beem
transmitted to the authorities of the Army and Mavy
" who will give as favorable consideration to this
matter as possible", but pointing out that Japan has
the right to land troops in the Settloment and to moor
warships at the present position(see Embassy's telegram
No. 44 of February 6, Noon).
B. Reaction in Japan to the Secretary's
letter to Mr. Borah.*
The publication of the Secretary's letter to Mr.
Borah occasioned wide comment in Japan . The letter was
axd
criticized with considerable heat in the Japanese press,
50P.

Japan. A statement said to have emanated from the
Foreign Office took issue with the Seoretary's letter.
This statement pointed out that Japan has no intention
17
of modifying the existing treaties but, on the contrary,
in order to realize the spirit of the treaties, has
been compelled to take aotive steps; that no treaty
prohibits the right of self proteotion; that the
Secretary was mistaken in regard to the part the re-

making of the Nine-Power Treaty.

C. Rumors of American Boyeott.
The agitation of an e conomie boyoott of Japan im
certain quarters of America has caused serious concerm and
some resentment in Japan. The Japanese have a practical
knowledge of the effeots of boyootts, and have reason
to dread a measure of this kind. The press reports
that a meeting of bankers was held in Tokyo on the 85th
to discuss economic matters, at which considerable attention
was given to the boycott agitation in America. A number
of newspapers have commented editorially on the dangers
to American-Japanese relations attending any attempt to
apply an economic boycott. The inrluential JIJI SHIMPO
states that the resolution of certain Americans for an
economio blookade is really a kind of appeal for a
declaration of war, and asks if those responsible have
considered that such an attitude will bring about a wer
between Japan and America, perhaps a world war.
II. POLITICAL RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES:
A. Process of the Shanghai Affair.
The month of February opened with Japanese marines
793.
in Shanghai engaged in desultory fighting with Chinese
soldiers and irregular troops. Unable to take effective
action against the overwhelming number of Chinese troops
massed in the vicinity of the city, the Japanese attaoked
the Chinese positions with bombing planes, and shelled
the Chinese fortifications at Woosung from the warships
in the Yangtze and Whangpoo rivers.
Ambassador Debuchi informed the Undersecretary on
February 2nd that no Japanese troops would be despatehed
to Shanghai to reinforee the marimes. On the same aay
the Cabinet decided to sead regular ammy troope to s

were in a position to Itrike a decisive blow against
the l9th Route Army.
On February 18 the Japanese Consul General
at Shanghai handed an ultimatum to Mayor Wu, demanding

B. Assassination of Mr. Inouye.
Mr. Junnosuke Inouye, twiee Minister of Finance
and an outstanding Minseito leader whose economie
policies formed the backbone of the Government during
the past two years, was shot dead on February 9. The
assassin, a young political maleontent, fired e pistol
at Mr. Inouye as he was arriving to speak at a politieal
moeting.

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
This outrage recalls the deaths of Mr.
and Mr. Hara, both statesmen of the highest type, whe
likewise were assassinated at the hands of misguided
patriots. Public sentiment is curiously tolerant of
these political assassins. Crimes committed with polisi-
cal motives are regarded as having extenuating eireum-
stances. The slayer of Hara is about to be released,
after eight years in prison , and the man who shot
Hamaguchi has not yet, sixteen months after the assault,
been oonvioted.
The death of Mr. Inouye was a severe blow to the
Minseito. He was the outstanding leader of that party
and campaign manager at the time of his death.

VI.
GENERAL FINANCIAL AND ECONOMIC CONDITIONS:
A.Fisheries Auction.
The annual auctien of fisheries was held at
Vladivostok on February 28th. According to the press
Japanese companies obtained 58 stations compared with
42 gained by the Soviet bidders, a gain of 7 per cent
for Japanese over the results of last year's auction.
A merger of Japanese fishing concerns interested
in the Kamohatka fisheries has been agitated for several
months. The press reports that the Oriental Development
Company has decided to loan the funds necessary for
effecting the merger, amounting to Yen 9,000,000. About
40 fishing concerns will be affected, chief of them being
the Niohiro Company.
B. Foreign Trade.
Preliminary figures of the Department of Finance show
an adverse trade balance of Yen 47,739,000 for February
and an adverse trade balance of Yen 95,062,000 for the two
months of this year.

A. Formal Opening of Embassy.
The formal opening of the new Embassy was held

B. Visit of Governor-General Roosevelt.
Governor General Roosevelt and his party arrived
in Tokyo on February l9th and were entertained as house
guests of the Ambassador. On February 2oth the Governor
General and his wife and daughter were received in audience
by and had luncheon with, the Emperor and Empress.

into the China situation arrived in Tokyo on February 29th,

REPORT ON CONDITIONS IN JAPAN
DURING THE MONTH OF MARCH, 1932.
I.
POLITICAL RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES:
Nothing to report.
II.
POLITICAL RELATIONS WITH OTHER COUNTRIES:
A. Formation of
Manchurian State *
Lr. Henry Pu-Yi, f rmer boy-eperor of China, was
formally inst llel
chief executive of the new State of
Vunchurit Chongchun on March 9. Attending the ceremony
vere Count Uchida, President of the South Manchurian
Railvay vho mude  coner tul tor. address, Lieutenant
General Honjo, Lieutenant General Mori, Mr. Tashiro the
Jap nese consul at Changchun, nd the Vice President of
the South Mnchuri Railvay, Ir. Eguchi. The local press
states that t ese pe sons ttended the ceremonies " in a V
porsonal capucity".
In answer to a circular telegram despatched on March
14th to various governments by the Foreign Minister of
the new State, the Japanese Foreign inister despatched
a conuaunicution to Mr. Tashiro, the Japanese Consul in
Changchun, to bo handed to the new government. This note,
according to the press, avoids any reference to the matter
of recognition and is limited to en expression of good will
toward the new government.
See Embassy's despatch No. 518 of March 10, 1932.
r

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.98)
2
Mr. Yoshizawa, the Foreign Minister, stated in the Diet.
in reply to an interpellation in regard to the new State:
" The Japanese Government is making a careful study of
the creution of the new Manchurian Stat e, its intention
being to extend formal recognition after taking into
consideration various circumstances. At the present
time the circunstances are such that the Government
reserves giving hast recognition". It should be re-
ebered that this stutement was not repared in advance
and was made v rbally in the course of a series of inter-
ellations.
B. Hostilities in Shanghai.
The Jap: nese drive against the 19th Route Army,
which had begun during the latter part of February with
the arrival of Japunese reinforcements, succeeded in
dealing a decisive blow to the Chinese on March 3rd.
The Noosung forts were finally reduced on that morning,
and the victorious Japanese swept the Chinese troops
out of Chapei, Tazang and Chenju, gaining control of a
20 kilometer area around Shanghai. An order for the
cessation of hostilivics gainst the Chinese was issued
on March 3rd by the Japanese military and naval commanders.
These events closed the campaign which began on February
20th, following the ultimatum demanding the withdrawal
of Chinese troops handed to the Mayor of Shanghai on
February 18th.
Japanese troops continued to be moved into the
Shanghai region until the middle of Marah, at which
time it is estimated that 55,000 treops had beem ton-
centrated. Troops have been gradually returnips..

=== Page 135 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
by the end of the month approximately 40,ooo remained
in Shanghai. T enty-two Japanese warships were reported
to have departed from Shanghai on March 2oth.
Figures given out by the War and Navy Departments
on March 15 give the total of casualties in the Manchurian
and Shanghai fighting as 3,744 or 768 killed and 2976
wounded.
C.
Peace Negotiations in Shanghai.
Formul negotiations for a cessation of Sino-
Japanese hostilities in the Shanghai area were opened at
a conference at Shanghai on March 24. The conference
secms to have opened in an atmosphere of pessimism, with
ccarcely c ncealed ill-feelins between the Jap nese and
Chinese delecates. The m in problem of the conference
was the mamer of  itharawal of the Jap nese troops. At
the time of .riting, little progress seens to have been
made in these negotiations, the views of Japanese and
Chinese representatives re mai ning idely at variance.
D. Soviet-Japanese Relations.
Reports vhich have emanated from time to time from
both Ja unese and Soviet sources regarding troop con-
centration on the Soviet-Japanese border have given rise
to some nervousness.*
Reports from Moscow told of
Japanese troop concentration, and reports reaching Tokyo
through official channels estimated that l00,00o Russian
troops
See Embassy's despatch No. 520 of March 10, 1932.

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
troops were massed in the Maritime Provinces.
Following conversations between the Japanese
Ambassador and Mr. Karakhan in Moscow on the 19th,
the Soviet Government published a communique summarising
the conversations, in which the two governments pledged
thomselves to strict observance of the Portsmouth Treaty.
This Treaty prohibits either country from taking military
measures  ich  uld menace the security of the boundary .
Mutual denials : ere made of troop c ncentrations or hostile
intent.
These conversations seem to h ve cleared the atmosphere
of a feeling of tension vhich has existed since the Japanese
military adv nce in no thern Mnchuria. The influential
JIJI SHIIPO stated on the 25th " The reports of the con-
versations in Moscow are reassuring ...... We believe that
the state onts by Mr. Hirota and Mr. Karakhan have dis-
pelled any uneasiness that may have been felt about relations
between the two countries".
E. Reports of Withdrawal From League.
There has been much talk, in recent weeks, of withdrawal
of Japan from membership in the League of Nations. From the
first the League's concern in the Sino-Jap nese dispute has
been unwelcome in Japan, and there seems to be a growing
sentiment in favor of withdrawal. The Tokyo NICHI NICHI
states: " It seems as though the time had arrived for Japan
to define her attitude toward the League. When the Council
listened to China and decided to a ply Article 15 of the
Covenant to the Sino-Jap nese dispute, the League gave Japan
oause

=== Page 137 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
cause to doubt whether her continued membership in
the organization would be of any value to her.
In all the discussion seen in the press, no
mention has been made of the disposition of mandate
territories, in the event of Japan's withdrawal from
the League.
III.
INTERNAL POLITICAL ACTIVITIES:
A. Diet Session.
The Sixty First Session of the Imperial Diet was
convened on March 18 and closed on March 24.* The Session
vas extraordinary und vas called for the purpose of passing
on emergency financial measures necessitated by the expenses
of the military oper tions in China. The Government, with
its l rge majority in the Diet, unticipated and encountered
little opposition to its measures. Those measures connected
With the Manchurian and Shanghai expeditions were, namely,
three Imperial Ordinances totaling Yen 69,910,000; a
sup lementary budget of Yen 7,579,161, for the fiscal year
just ended; and a supplementary budget of Yen 59,519,085
for the new fiscal year. In addition to these bills,
post-facto approval was given to the Imperial Ordinances
that prhibited the conversion of notes into gold and to
the Imperial Ordinance that limited to Yen 44,000,000 the
amount for redemption of principal of national loans to
be transferred to the sinking fund for the past fisoal
year in order to cover a deficit in the General Accounts,
while a second supptementary budget for the fisoal year
See Embassy's despatch No. 538 of March 26, 1932.

=== Page 138 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
just ended was passed to cover a deficit of Yon 1,422,471
in the pension account of the Department of Communications.
These measures are discussed in some detail in deppatoh
No. 533 of March 26, 1932.
B. Discord in the Cabinet.
The resignation of Mr. Nakahashi as Minister for
Home Affairs was ccepted b the Emperor on March 16th.
His resignation, ostensibly on cc unt of ill health,
was ctually in c nse uence of the Sakurada Mon Affair
894.
in January. According to Japanese ideas, Mr. Nakanash1
bore the onus of responsibility for the attempt on the
Emperor, ni the Cabinet feared that his continuance in
office. ould cause difficulties in the coming Diet session.
To fill this im ortant position, Dr. Suzuki, then
Hini ter of Railvays, was nominated by the Premier. Mr.
21.
Kavamur ,  Me ber of the Hou e of Peers, was nominated to
succeed Dr. Suzuki as Minister of Rail..ays. Dr. Suzuki's
nomination brought strong opposition from another party
leader, Mr. T konami, and his lieutenant Mr. Kuhara. The
appointmente were dela ed until after the close of the
Diet session, when, on March 25, they were submitted
to the Emperor and confirmed.
This dispute, the details of which are too com-
plicated to relate, is evidence to many observers of
the fundamental weakness of the Seiyukai Cabinet,
despite its recent victory in the general elections,
and indicates that it may not have long to live. There
has recently been much talk of a super-party cabinet
which will have sufficient strength to confront and
solve the manifold problems now facing the natiom

=== Page 139 === (confidence 0.98)
-7-
However, since the confirmation of the appointment of
Suzuki and Kawamura, the subject seems to have been
dropped by the press.
C. Assassination of Baron Dan.
The public, frightened by the assassination of
Mr. Inouye on F bruary 9th, was shocked by the death of
Baron Dr. Takuma Dan on March 5th at the hand of a
Jan
youthful reactionary by the name of Goro Hishinuma.
Baron Dan was senior managing director of the Mitsui Gomei
Kaisha and the outst nding leader of Japanese industry
and finance. The  urderer who was captured on the spot,
proved to be a nutive of the sane village from which
the assassin of Mr. Inouye came. This coincidence led
to an invustigation b. the police that revealed the ex-
istence of an org nized " blood brotherhood" sworn to kill
m ny of the most prominent persons in Japan, among them
Baron Goh, Baron Makatsuki, r. Tokonami, the present
Minister of Railways , und t least four other unnamed
persons.
At the time of writing thirteen men have been
taken by the police and charged with complicity in the
murder plot. The self-confessed leader of the brother-
hood is s id to be a priest, Nissho Inouye, but certain
indications point to the complicit. of other persons
of high st nding. It is reported that the pistols used
by the murderers of both Dan and Inouye were obtained
through a Lieutenant Fujii, who was killed during the
fighting in China. The press makes vague references to
" certain prominent leaders of patriotic organizations",
in discussing the crimes. The motives of the murderers
is uncertain but seems to have been inspired by a kind of
misguided

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.98)
-8
misguided patriotism instigated probably by persons
.of influence.

the Diet in regard to hat the Government proposed to

do deal with the "reign of terror in the capital".

GENERAL FINANCIAL AD ECONOMIO CONDITIONS:
A. Excess of Imports.
Japan's visible foreign trade for the first
quarter of 1932 resulted in un excess of imports in the
8
amount of "en 159,002,000, showing a gain of Yen 122,080,000
over the corresponding period of lust  ear. Exports for
the period anounted to en 251,688,000 against Yen 293,428,000
for the year before, and imports were Yen 410,690,000
against en 330,350,000. The figures for the month
of March show in increase of 4.9 per cent in exports and
and increase of 30.1 p r cent in imports over the year
before. By far the largest item contributing to the
excess of imports is raw cotton, large stocks of vwhich
have been purchased in anticipation of further drops in
the exchange.
B. Commodity prices falling.
In spite of the much advertised inflation
policy of the Seiyukai Government, commodity prices
have consistently fallen, after the first flurry
following the reimposition of the gold embargo.
Commodities

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.98)
-9-

exchange rate rose between 20 and 30 per cent imme-

to lack of consumption, have tended to fall. Last year
per koku
the average price of rice was Yen 18 50., Recent quota-
tions average round Yen 24., which show a gain for the
furmer, but increased cost of fertilizers will largely
offset this guin.

new Manchurian Stute.

B.
Activities of Japanese in Manchuria.
1.
Military Operations.
The Japanese troops in anchuria were kept busy
throu hout the month of ay endeavoring to subdue rebellious
armies and seemingly innumerable bands of bandits.
Small
bodies of anti-lanchukuo troops reportedly instigated and
encouraged by Chang sueh-liang made trouble throughout
Lanchuria endancering the lives and property of Japanese
residents to an even creater extent seemingly than before
September, 1931.
The actual areas over which the Japanese
troops had control were not increased to any appreciable
extent and re orts fron fo: in observers as well as some
Japanese, incluain .r. Tanaka, apanese mbassador to
Moscow, and Jount Uchide, resiuent of the South Manchuria
Railway,
renot eeur in
Traffic on th Suncari River and on the eastern branch
of te chinese astern Railroad was practically stopped in
spite of strenuous efforts on the part of Japanese and
Manchukuo troo to clear these important channels of trade.
There had been indications that the Japanese had been con-
iderably disappointed in the lack of effectiveness and
loyalty on the part of the Manchukuo troops, which necessi-
tated lar er forces of Japanese.
Reports reaching Japan were fairly unanimous in in-
dicating that Chang Hsueh-liang is far from inactive in
stirring up trouble in Lanchuria and one unconfirmed report
claimed that he was preparing a direct attack on Lanchuria

through Shanhaikwan.

have centered around angcheng east of Harbin, Liuho.
