the forces against which the government. was operating
tended to avoid clashes with larce bodies of Japanese
troops and confined their attention to isolated detach-
ments.
. In every engacement,according to press reports
reachin Japan, the rebels suffered considerable losses
and were scattered only to regather again somewhere else.
It is reported that the Japanese military proposed a plan
for the unification of authority in Manchuria combining the
military, consular and South anchuria Railway organizations
under a single authority. The press in Japan became more
and more impatient with the failure of the military to
succeed in their projects and expressed a desire to seo
the new government take more definite steps towards the
putting into operation of a definite plan in Manchuria.
2. General Plans.
The Saito cabinet seems not to have formulated any
new plan with regard to Japanese relations with the new
state of Manchukuo. At one time, about the middle of
May, it was reported in the press that the army authorities
were in favor of immediate recognition of the new state.
Whether or not they were still of that opinion at the
end of the month was uncertain. They continued however
to demand a strong, positive policy towards Manchuria,
whioh seemed to mean prinoipally the sending of more
troops for the purpose of pacifying the country. This
polioy naturally required demands for funds whioh it was
decided would be asked for as soon as the Diet was eom-
vened.

stance of a  ress report from Mukden, dated May 31st. The
reasons for his decision were not known.

C.
Soviet-Japanese Relations.
The tension between Japan and Soviet Russia, which
14
grew alarmincly during the month of April, appeared to
have considerably eased off during Lay. Among the reasons
for this would appear to be the reassurance of Japan that
Russia did not intend to take any immediate forceful means
of interfering with the extension of Japanese influence in
northern Manchuria. Also the difficulties which the Japanese
troops in northern Manchuria encountered tended to eliminate
the possibility of their taking offensive measures against
the Soviet troops which had reportedly been mobilizing near
the Lanchurian border. The inability of the Japanese to
rmake any obvious headway in their program of pacifying
manchuria seemed to have relieved the Soviet of their
earlier worries.
The fisheries dispute between the Soviets and Japan
approached an agreement but no final settlement was reached.
No headway was made in the Soviet desire for a non-aggression

principal army leaders to continue in office. Both
Suzuki and ex-Premier Wakatsuki proved unwilling to
join the new cabinet.
Takahashi was induced to remain
in office and it therefore became practically certain
that his financial policies would be continued. For
further information regarding the present make-up of the
cabinet see Embassy's despatch No. 635 of June 2, 1932.
C.
The general nolitical situation as of May 3lst.
The terroristic acts of May 15th brought about a
certain amount of revulsion of feeling against "Fascism".
It seemed to make the political parties, the newspapers,
and the eluer statesmen of the Privy Council, and the
Imperial Household, more determined than ever that con-
stitutional covern cnt should be supported.
The formation
of a national non-political cabinet, the admitted eakness
of the Cabinet and the tremendous size of the difficulties
with which it must necessarily have to face were not re-
assuring.
The attitude of he Seiyukai, Minseito, and
Army leaders was reported to be cne of watchful waiting.
All were willine to cive the present government temporary
support but there was no confidence that the cabinet would
be able to last long.
For this reason the acknowledged
leaders of the inseito and Seiyukai parties were reported
to be unwilline to join the cabinet as they wished to be
free to form a new cabinet in case the present one should
fail.
All factions, with the exception of the Army leaders,
had expressed absolute disapproval of "direct action"
methods

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.98)
-14-
methods and the presence of a large number of sympathizers
among the younger officers caused considerable worry in
many quarters and led a number of observers to express
the opinion that assassinations had only begun and that
if the military could not stop their younger officers
from committing further dastardly acts similar to those
of Lay l5th that there were almost sure to be acts of re-
venge.

I. RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED STATES.
A. Arrival of the American Ambassador.
The arrival of Ambassador Grew in Japan on June 6*
was the occasion for the publication by the principal
123G841
newspapers, both Japanese and foreign, of several accounts
extending a cordial welcome to the new Ambassador and his
family and expressing sentiments of friendship for the
country he represents. The ceremony of presentation of
his credentials to is Inperial njesty the Emperor was
animated by the same spirit**.
The societies and in-
stitutions of Tokyo which ure identified with Americans
and things A erican also joined in welcoming the new envoy.
On June 21 a dinner was civen in his honor by the
Acrica-Japan ociety.
His Excellency Prince Tokugawa
presided and addresses were delivered by the chairman,
by Viscount Ishii, and by the Ambassador. The press
has curried extensive corments on the speeches of the
last two named. Viscount Ishii's remarks, which have al-
ready been reported to the Department***, have been gen-
erally characterized by the Japanese press as a friendly
but frank statement of the Japanese viewpoint upon the
relations between the two countries. He stated that
each nation has its share of activity and the only danger
of war between the countries lay in the possibility of
one of them interfering with the other in its sphere.

A. American, British, French and Italian Envoys in
conference with remier.
On June 8 the Ambassadors of Great Britain, France,
and Italy, and the Chargé d'Affaires of the United States
called on the Prenier in his capacity of Foreign Minister.*
Althouch no statement was issued to the public concerning
the subject of discussion the press generally assumed that
it concerned the calling of a conference concerning
Shanghai. While it was understood that the four powers
had rejected Japan's proposal for a preliminary confer-
ence of the Powers, excluding China, the ASAIII stated that
France and Italy were more agreeable to the proposition
than were Great ritain and the United States.

against General Ia and the bandits. The status of all

C. French Embassy's statement concerning the son-
vention governing visits of aviators to Japan.
In the JAPAN ADVERTISER of June 22 there was printed
a communication from the French Embassy in Tokyo calling
attention to the paper's error in stating in a previous
issue that a French aviator, Viscount de Sibour, who in-
tends flying to Japan, would, through his Embassy in Tokyo,
request of the Japanese Government permission to fly over
Japanese territory.
. The communication asserted that the
statenent was incorrect; that, inasmuch as Japan is a
signatory to the Convention of Aerial Navigation signed
at Paris on October 13, 1919, according to which the con-
tracting parties agree to accord to the aviators belonging
to the nationality of the other contracting parties the
right of free passage over their territories without
having to ask permission of the government of the country
over which they intend to fly, a French aviator does not
have to ask peraission to fly over Japanese territory,
but must notify the authorities of his intention to fly
over Japanese territory and observe the regulations for
such flichts set up by the Japanese government.
conmunication from the French Embassy goes on to state
that "during past years the Japanese Government has system-
atically ignored this accord, and the representatives of
many foreign powers which are parties to the convention
have neglected to remind the Japanese Government of this
agreement".
According to the third vOlume of TREATIES, CONVENTIONS,
ETC., BETWEEN TIE UNITED STATES AIND OTHER POWERS the boVe-
mentioned convention, though signed by many powers inolud-
ing the United States, has been ratified only by the
Br1tish

Portugal, Yugoslavia, and Siam, with the subsequent adheron
of Nicaragua, Peru, Liberia, and Persia.
D. Japan's relations with Soviet Russia.
There has been no outstanding development during the

There are rumors that the fisheries dispute is on the
心

. If this is true one of the prin-

in this connection reports of more cooperation between the

An American member of a firm which sells American

C
ONEHDENTHAL

The faot that
Japanese will buy such obligations would seem to indicate
that there is a belief among some of them that peace
will continue on the northern border.
The local press gave some prominence to a meeting of
the Russo-Japanese Association which met in Tokyo on June
1l. It was attended by Premier Visoount Mikoto Saito,

of whom are directors of the Association, and the Russian
Ambassador. There was an interohange of friendly speeches
such as might characterize any meeting for the furthering
of relations between two countries.

III. INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS.
In the field of internal affairs the 62nd session of
the Imperial Diet overshadowed the other news during the
early part of the month of June. The important matters
upon which attention was focused during its sessions were
farm relief, tariff reform, monetary reform and exohange
control, the budget, and Manchurian policy.
Since its
adjournment on the fifteenth of the month political speou-
lation has centered around the appointment of a new Minister
for Foreign Affairs, the probable activities of Ir. Adachi
and his friends in forming a third party, and the disputes
between the tvo  rincipal parties over the "spoils" of
office, particularly the occupancy of the posts of Prefect-
ural governors.
There have been many conments in the news-
papers attacking the present political parties and calling
upon the present covernnent to purify politics.
In many quarters the Government is criticized for
inactivity but on the other hand there is a widespread
realization of the enormity of its problems and a feeling
that it should be civen more of an opportunity to prove
itself.
On the 17th of the month there were reports of the
impending resignation of Finance inister Takahashi but
he denied them the next day.
A. The 62nd Session of the Imperial Diet.
The sixty-second extraordinary session of the Diet
was opened on June 1 in the presence of His Majesty the
Emperor and was closed on June 15th with Premier Visoount
Saito reading the Imperial rescript ordering the adjourn-
ment.

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.98)
- 10 -
ment.
Its accomplishments consisted of the passage of
three budget bills, nineteen government bills, and two
important lower house resolutions, which are disoussed
in another report from this Embassy*.
On June 3 Prenier Viscount Saito made two addresses,
the first in his capacity of Premier and the second as
Foreign Linister**.
Copies of both these speeches are
enclosed.
The first reaction of a large section of the
Japanese press was to deplore the  remier's failure to lay
dowm the definite policies whicn the Covernment would pur-
sue in dealing with the problcms confronting the country.
Hoever, after a lapse of several days conment became
 ore favorable to the renier, it being said that he had
inspired the public with confidence that his cabinet would
study the problems and come to the best solution possible.
On the openine day of the Diet General Araki, the War
.inister, expressed his profound recret that eleven military
cadets vere involved in the assassination of Premier Inukai
and offered his sincere apologies for his insufficient ef-
forts to prevent such ha penings.
In defense of his re-
tention of office he said that he had pondered over his
course very deeply and had finally concluded that his duty
in the present momentous situation lay in continuing in
office in defiance of all adverse criticisms so that he can
devote his best service to the state.
Such an abject
apology seems not to have been expected from an army of-
ficer trained in the Japanese military code and his sup-
porters have not failed to point out the qualities of
high character displayed by the general in remaining in
office and accepting moral responsibility for the ineident
instead of resigning or even committing suicide, for whi
he would have had many precedents.

IV. COURSE OF LEGISLATION.
The Diet Session.
The sixty-second session of the Imperial Diet was
convened on June 1 and ended on June 15.
The session was
extraordinary, and was called to consider the supplementary
budget and other emergency measures. Budgetary estimates
for the fiscal year 1932-1933 were passed. Nineteen bills,
lMostly of a financial nature, were passed, including a new
tariff bill. Two resolutions of some significance were
passed by the Lower House; one calling for the convocation
of an extraordinary session of the Diet to consider relief
measures for the distressed farming classes; and another
calling upon the Government to extend immediate recognition
to anchukuo. All of these measures are discussed in
another despatch of this Embassy.*

Translation.
ADIIRAL VISCOUNT SAITO'S SPEECH AS PREMIER
IN TIIE HOUSE OF PEERS JUNE 3. 1932.
It is a matter of extreme regret for the State that
Premier Inukai died in an unexpected disaster just before
the convening of the 62nd Diet.
At this time when the situation is fraught with many
difficulties, I received the Imperial command to form the
new Cabinet. Awestricken by this unexpected command I
sought cooperation in various quarters and organized the
so-called national Cabinet with the object of coping with
the situation with a view to respectfully undertake to
assume the heavy responsibility I was called upon to bear
and thereby to fulfill my humble duty to the Throne.
After I have just taken up my post, I deem it a great
honor that I am civen an opportunity to explain the polioy
of the new Cabinet before you now.
As regards the foreign policy, it goes without saying
that the new Cabinet respects international fidelity and
maintains the traditional policy to endeavor to contribute
to the progress and development of the human race in co-
operation with the other Powers.
At the same time, I think it unavoidable that we take
an independent position to defend our rights and interests
in line with international justice.
I take this opportunity to report briefly on the situ-
ation in China. At Shanghai, the armistice agreement was
signed between the Japanese and Chinese armies on May 5.
This is a matter for mutual congratulation, and I sinoere-
ly hope the situation in that region will become more and
more peaceful hereafter.
No

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Next, in Manchuria, the new State inaugurated early.
in March is gradually developing in good shape, and I
deem it a matter of immense importance not only for the
restoration and enhancement of peace and order and prosper-
ity in that region but also for the establishment of peace
in the Orient at large that the new nation shall continue
to develop steadily. I am convinced that our people are
all looking forward to the future of the new nation with
great expectation.
I avail myself of this opportunity to express our
sincere anpreciation of the hardships our military and
naval forces in anchuria and the Shanghai region have
undergone, and I express our deep sympathy with the army
and navy officers, the diplomatic officials and the leading
residents who suffered personal injuries at Shanghai on
April 29 at the hands of some lawless elements.
The provailing situation is so grave that the people
characterize it as extraordinary.
The intense economic
depression, which has so far shown no sign of early improve-
ment, spurred by the destitution of the rural populace and
the stagnation in municipal life, threatens to become worse
and worse.
What is worse still, radical demonstrations continue
to take place one after another and the people are seized
with extreme uneasiness. We cannot but deem the situation
as a really crave crisis.
To wipe out this uneasiness from among the people and
to help stabilize the national life is what those in the
Government are required to do even at the risk of their
lives in the face of all difficulties.
In other words, we ought to endeavor to keep striot
peace and order against all unlawful acts, while at the
same time considering measures to alleviate the difficulties
in

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
in financial circles and to relieve unemployment, renovate
the agricultural villages with a view to stabilizing the
life of the masses of the people.
This is a matter for the most urgent attention at the
present, and it is one of the important missions of the
new Cabinet.
Regarding the recent deplorable incident, I extremely
regret to hear criticism about military discipline. It is
needless to say that all operations of the Imperial army
ought to be coverned by nothing but the Imperial command,
and t e fundamental principle of the organization of the
Imperial ariy requires the troops to observe loyalty,
propriety, valor, faithfulness and simplicity with an un-
alterable sincerity under strict discipline. The prin-
ciple is what the masses of the people have long looked
up to as the glory of the Imperial army.
I have no doubt that all of the officers and men of
our arnmy and navy will unite in endeavoring to observe
the principles as laid dovm in the Imperial Precepts for
the Army and Navy with a view to responding to His Majesty's
wishes by safeguarding the clory of the Imperial army for-
ever.
To purify political circles and to remove their in-
veterate evils is another of the important missions re-
quiring our special attention at present.
It is quite natural and adnits of no doubt that political
parties oppose each other and fight for their respective
political policies under constitutional rule. Such a
system, if operated properly, will help in controlling
public opinion and facilitate deliberation on political
measures, thereby making the operation of the government
of the State fair and just.
However,

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
However, political strife is often apt to have un
desirable effects on the central and provinoial adminiotra
tion and to cause various evils in connection with the
elections. And we can not be certain that such a possibilit
will not assume such grave proportions as to make the
people entertain misgivings about the future of constitution-
al government.
In view of the crave situation now besetting the
nation, the new Cabinet is organized on the basis of the
principle knovm as nationwide cooperation instead of de-
pending on any single political party, but it is needless
to say that the Cabinet respects the legislature.
also do not make little of the political parties.
However, the new Cabinet will make every effort to
remove the evils of the political parties and is ready
to use its wholehearted sincerity and enthusiasm in an
effort to win the confidence of the people tomorrow after
overcoming the unfavorable criticism directed against us
today with a view to meeting the heartfelt aspirations of
the nation for the renovation of political circles.
In short, I believe our country is faced with a
serious situation, politically, economically, internation-
ally, and also as regards its thoughts. Consequently,
in addition to the principles I have previously mentioned,
there are many items that must be executed, including the
development of our industries, the reformation of our
education, and the proper guidance of thoughts.
A11
these it is easy to talk about, but difficult to execute.
In order to solve our present difficulties, we im-
mediately intend to draw up necessary practical counter-
measures, starting with the simpler issues first, and
then

=== Page 202 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
then tackling the harder ones, and gradually put them
into operation, and with the cooperation of the entire
nation we plan to institute measures suitable for the
present situation.
As regards the various measures studied by the
former government, we are on the whole adopting those
we find useful, particularly the emergency measures for
saving our economic circles from their present uncertainty,
and we are presenting them e bodied in the additional
budget and the vurious bills, for your approval.
latters not requiring inclusion in the budget or in
the bills, we propose to curry then into effect gradually,
and as regards other policies other than those introduced in
the present Diet we intend to introduce them in the next
recular session of the Diet.
In this manner we hope to
carry out our creat task of coping with the present serious
situation, and to relieve His lajesty's anxiety, and live
up to the anticipations of the nation. We earnestly ask
your approval of our objects, and request you to pass the
neasures without delay.

(b) For ation of ir. Adachi 's party.
Ir. Adachi, who caused the dowmfall of the Iinseito
Cabinet last December has continued his efforts to organize
a new party.
He claims that he can now command enough
votes in the Diet to receive recognition for his party.
If this is the case the Linseito party will be seriously
weakened.
The aims of the party as outlined by Ir. Adachi are
the establishment of the principles of international
justice, stabilization of the cost of living, and the
establishment of popular government.
The leader has so
far been silent on how these aims will be realized.

As the present government is a coalition one and as

(c) Fusion of Tvo Proletarian Parties.*
On July 24 the Shakai Iinshuto (Social Democratio
Party) and the Zenkoku Rono Taishuto (Hational Labor-Farmer
Party) were merged into the new Shakai Taishuto (Socialist
Peoples' Party).
This move strengthens the proletarian
movenent as these vere the two leading proletarian parties
and both of the have been losing votes in recent eleotions.
(a) Peers Choose their Diet Representatives.
For the seventh time since the Imperial declaration
of l889 which created the Japanese Diet, the lower orders
of Japan's nobility met on July lo to elect their repre-
sentatives in the House of Peers. All princes and
larquises are automatically menbers of the House but every
seven years 18 scunts, 6 viscounts, and 66 barons are
elected from among those ranks of nobility.

years are Jount Yasutoshi Yanagisawa, statistical authority;

secretary's address of August 8 before the
Councl of Foreign Relations, was either to
stir upanti-American feeling or else to bring
himselfone step nearer the post of Foreign
Minister

to distin,uish between the Secretary's state-
ment and thet of President Hoover was not

unable to discovy difference between the
points of view of .ne two statements (page 2).

declaration of a "Monroe Doctrine" for Asia.

(b) Influence of the Military in Internal Affairs.
at the present time.

894.00

a more prominent rart in intermal affairs. As the soldiers
come principally from the rural districts where conditions
are bad there undoubtedly exists among the rank and file.
serious opposition to the political leaders whon they believe
to be controlled by the financial interests. The prophets

against the industrial population or the cities. However.
those who follow this line of reasoning too closely are apt

(c) Mejji Sugar Company Scandal.
The alleged scandal of tax evasion by the Moiji duger
Manufacturing Company has attracted no little attention
during the month of August. The company is accused of having
sold imported sugar out of its bonded warehouse without the
payment of duty, or with the payment of a low duty for a lower
grade of sugan than that actually imported. Irregularities
were discovered early in the sum er but publicity was giyen.
to the case when upon the a proach of the Diet session there
appeared to be a divergence of opinion between the Finance
Department and the Department of Justice as to the amount
of fine that should be collected from the offending company
According to press reports an ag nt of the Department of
Justice found tie company liaule to a fine totaling
Yen 41, 000,c00; Whereas the Finance Depa tment compelled
the company to pay only Yen 720,00o. A political opponent
of Finance Minister Takahashi filed with the Tokyo Procu-
rator a letter of accusation ugainst him, charging him with
breach of trust . Almost an entire day's session of the
Diet was dey ted to questioning the Government in the matter;
the Finance Minister stated that a thorough investigation
was being mude and all guilty noreens we ld be punished.
(a) Aotivities of lir. Koken 1an ka.
On August 9 the Japanese press of Tokyo gave much
prominence to the activities of the 90 year-old Mr. Koken
Tanaka, formerly Count Koken anaka, who under the aperor
Meiji was for 20 years Minister of the Imperial Household
Department. Mr. anaka , appearing in Tokyo, held a series
of interviews wit me bers of the Household Department The
newsbaper

newspaper accounts of the interviews wore given an air of
mystery by the use of blank circles which stood for names
which the papers could not print(Probably the names were
those of members of the Imperial Fa ily). The press announce-
ment stated that the mission of Mr. Tanaka was very grave
and had to do with the respons pility of Dr. Kitokuro Ikki
Minister of the Imperial Household und Count Mobuaki Makino.
Lord Keeper of the Privy Seal, for " past occurrences" the
nature of which ere not revealed. Mr. Tanaka was quoted as
saying that "the time has come for me to do this last service
for the nation".
Many rumors have been cireulating regarding the meaning
of all these activities and vague statements. All the stories
arrive at the point that an attaapt was made by the fascist
and military e ements to force the resignations of Dr. Ikki and
Count Makino whom they consider t o conservative in their in-
fluence over Imperial Household affairs. An at empt was made
on Count Makino's life on May 15 when Premier Inukai was
assassinated but fort mately he was not at home when the
assassins called. In this new attempt to get rid of him and
Dr. Ikki, Mr. Tanaka was persuaded(some say "b ught") to try
to place upon them the responsibility for certain "past oe
currences". The true m ture of these "past occurrences" is
unknown though the have been the subject of much speculation.
If the affair was an attempt to remove the two Ministers from
office, it was a definite failure.

COURSE OF LEGISLATION
The 63rd session of the Japanese Diot,
an Imperial rescript of August 5, which was callmd
exclusively with relief measures, was opened on Aud
94.032
It was summoned for a nine-day session but as the doter
tion of the opposition made long discussions inovitable is
was soon apparent that the bills could not be passed in
the allotted tias and the session was extended.
No important legislation was actually passed before
the end of the month. The first four days were devoted
to the formal opening by His Majesty the Emperor, getting
organized, and listening to the speeches of the Premier
and the Foreign Minister.* The last days of the month were
spent in heated discussions between the Government, on the
one side, and a very large faction of the majority party,
the Seiyukai, and the new National League, on the other side.
The oppositionists attacked the Government's budget, ad-
voca ed changes in the Rice and Cereals Law and opposed the
Government's project for read justment of farmers debts.
(The Diet session is being thorou  ly treated in a separate
report from this Embassy).

Relations with "Manchukuo" since recognition
Since recognition by Japan there have been mary rumor
of coming changes in the Japanese advisers of the Manchukuo
regime. Recognition was delayed partly because of Japan's
difficulties in managing the new government and there were
many reports that the Japanese subjects attached to the new
regime had ideas of their own and would not submit to diet
tion from Tokyo. For this reason it would not be surprising

in the personnel. Ir. Tokuzo Komai, the chief of the General
Affairs Bureau of the Executive Yuan, according to some re-
ports will be the first to go. Among new appointees in Man-

to these suspicions since the Soviets began to act as inter
modiaries in obt ining the releuse of the Japanese women and
children.

=== Page 360 === (confidence 0.98)
POLITICAL RELATIONB WITE THE ONID
During the month of November the od
mmese aotivity in foreigd affairs shirted from Tokyo
to Geneva and there is espesially very little td redord
regarding her relations with the United States. The
anti-American press campaign had practically ceased
the beginning of the month and there has been no more
evidence of it. This does not necessarily mean that the
anti-American sentiment has died out but only that the
efforts of the more farsighted Japanese officials which
were reported last month have been so far successful. The
personnel of the press bureau of the Ministry of War has
recently been completely changed and little has been
heard from Mr. Shiratori of late. However, the efforts
of the Japanese officials to stem the tide of anti-
Americanism have been helped by various other factors.
In the first place nothing has been said or done to excite
hostility. Secondly, the recognition of "Manchukuo" by
Japan afforded the public a sense of complaisant satis-
faction that the main issue between the United States
and Japan had from the Japanese point of view been
finally settled by that step. Thirdly, industrial
conditions are improving in Japan, at least temporarily,
and a feeling of optimism prevails in industrial circlés.
Nevertheless, latent hostility towards the United States
exists and would probably flare up at any provocation.*
*Embassy's telegram No. 275, November 28-8 p.m.

=== Page 361 === (confidence 0.98)
tos and Japan is unimaginable unless une
ot war alone." He might have added

purse is ampty there is little likelihood opa

atata that the presence of the American, fleet in the

The truce which the press has called in its belligeren
attacks on the United States is evidenced even in the tomm
on the proceedings of the League of Nations. The old
charge that the United States is forcing Europe to oppose
Japan is now seldom seen in the editorial columns, though

alleged connection between the attitude of France on the
Manchurian question and the American debt policy.
The Japanese press appeared in general to welcome
the outcome of the American presidential election.*

12/

tions will lead to the conclusion that the low prices of goods
manufactured in Japan are due to the fall of the yen and not
to "dumping", if by that term is meant selling below the cost
of production or below the prices at which the goods are sold
in other markets. As will be seen from the enclosed articles,
some resentment is felt at the procedure of the American
Customs officials.

POBITTEAL RELATIONS WITH OTWER COUNTHIND
(a) Janant and the League of Nationa
During the month of November the attention of
thoge Japanese who are coneerned with foreign affairs
concentrated upon Geneva and the principal Japanese
dulegate, Mr Matsuoka. There is a general feeling of
confidence in Mr. Matsuoka and the public is backing him
wholeheartedly. It might be said that for the first tim
since September 18, 1931, the Japanese feel that their
case is being ably presented by their diplomats abroad.
Mr. Matsuoka has been exempted from the widespread criticism
in Japan of the personnel of both the Foreign Office and the
Japanese diplomatic service.
As was expected the observations of the Japanese
Government on the Lytton Report took issue with the as-
sertion that the Japanese troops exceeded the legitimate
limits of self-defense in their operations on the night of
September 18, 1931 and with the accusation that "Manchukuo"
was set up by the Japanese. This stand has been strongly
supported by the Japanese public and press. Furthermore,
there has been no indication of any disposition on the part
of the Japanese to enter into a plan for settling the matter
that does not accept Japan's recognition of "Manchukuo".
Subject to t e very imoortant and all-inclusive reserva-
tions, the attitude of official and public opinion might
be said to have been conciliatory. The HOCHI SHIMBUN
on November 15, referring to the difference between the
viewpoints

=== Page 365 === (confidence 0.99)
apan and the League, stated that
basible to decide which is corredt. The par
asts that the world await developments iu Mancha
bafore passing judgment; if Japan succeeds in establishin
order and "Manchukuo" gains the adherence of the peopley
then Japan's action will be vindicated. As a means to this
end the newspapers at that date looked with favor upon the
idea of a commission of conciliation, presumably to be set
up by the Council of the League of Nations. Several other
newspapers appeared to be friendly to the idea because it
would be a means of keeping the matter out of the hands or
the Assembly. When it appeared that the Council would
possibly refer the Manchurian affair to the Assembly before
the end of the month there was a churus of protests from
Tokyo and threats were heard that Japan would withdraw
her delegation from the Assembly if the representatives of
the smaller powers should be too "insulting" in their re-
marks.
On November 28 the Foreign Office Spokesman after
stating that Japan would never compromise on the matter
of her own recognition of "Manchukuo", said that Japan
would welcome a resolution of the As embly which would
bind the other nations not to extend recognition for the
time being. "Japan would rather have the stage set before
the guests arrive". He expressed the hope that the League
ould delay its final decision until Japan has established
order

(b) Japanese Relations mith "Manchukuo.
There has been little evidence in Tokyo of activity
in Japanese-Manchukuo relations during the month. Itis
reported that the Foreign Office has drafted a treaty
whioh has to be submitted to other departments of the
Japanese Government before being negotiated with "Manchukio"
It provides for gradual abolition of extraterritoriality
and. some adjustment in customs matters.
During the first part of the month Japanese agents
wwere in Soviet territory negotiating with the rebels at
Manchuli for the release of the Japanese men who were stili
detained there. However, the rebel leaders were reported
to have refused to negotiate and therefore the Japanese
prepared to attack with force. It was alleged that the
Japanese had intercepted a telegram from Mr. T.V. Soong,
Finance Minister of the Nanking Government, to General
Su Ping-wen, the leader of the rebels, in which the latter
was advised not to harm the Japanese whom he held, as
world opinion might turn against him if they should suffer.
With this assurance, the Japanese troops launched an attack
on November 20. The same day 42 Japanese prisoners were
reported to have been released by the rebels. As the month
closed it appeared that the Japanese forces were advancing
and might soon subdue the rebels.

JAL PODTTICAL AFFAIRS
The Arreut or Young Toyama and other
reactionary conspirators"
November was an active month in the field of1
palitics. The most sensational happening, and a very;
niricant one, was the arrest and detention on November
In Tokyo of a gang of about 30 young reactionaries am
whom were Hidezo Toyama, son of the famous Mitsuru Toyama
and Yoshi Kodama, a notorious leader, who incidentally
the person who stole the flag from the American Embassy
compound in 1926, at the time of the agitation over the
Immigration Act. These young"radical reactionaries"
were acoused of implication in the assassinations of the pist
year and of planning another series. It is understood that
among the intended victims were General Ugaki, Bovernor
General of Chosen; Count Makino, Lord Keeper of the Privy
Seal; Mr. Ikki, Minister of the Imperial Household; Premier
Saito; Mr. Takahashi, Minister of Finance; Baron Go of the
Tokyo Electric Light Company, Mr. S. Ikeda, President of
the Mitsui Bank; and Baron Hiranuma, President of the Privy
Council. The plot was to have been carried out during the
annual Army Manoeuvres which took place at Osaka from November
11 to 14.
The police must have been in possession of very strong
evidence against this gang, especially young Toyama, to have
arrested them. The Toy ma family have been looked upon by
a
Embassy's despatch No. 197 of November 17, 1932.

=== Page 369 === (confidence 0.98)
-10
 section of the public as super-patriots
froldent is undoubtedly a sign of the growing strength
of the saner and more liberal persons in the Government,
s the police could hardly have made these arrests without
support or the highest officials of the Government,
Including the Minister of War.
There is no direct evidence of the complicity of the
younger military officers in this most recent plot. However
they have in the past been associated with the members of
the Fascist-reactionary organizations. Furthermore, during
recent months there has been circulated among the younger
army officers a strongly worded document calling for action
Kohusy
against General Ugaki and Count Makino.*
The object of the conspirators in seeking to slay the
leaders of the country was to rid the people of leaders whom
they considered bad and to abolish the parliamentary system.
The men implicated are mostly very young and imbued with im-
mature political ideas. It is significant that the reaction
of the public has been decidedly against this conspiracy.
At the time of the May 15 incident there was a very definite
undercurrent of sympathy with the conspirators on the part
of the public but there has been no evidence of this during
the exposure of the recent plot.

Dissatisfaction with the present Government is due to
its budget for 1937-4, now practically completed. Its
unprecedented size has seriously alarmed the economists and
industrial le ders of Japan. However, the Seiyukai, which
is the majority party in the Diet and could therefore bring
about the downfall of the present Cabinet, has no alternative
to offer. Furthermore, no plan can ignore the real cause
of the budget exoansion, which is the demands of the military,
nor is anyone willing at present to oppose these demands.
The Seiyukai hs never been satisfied with a situation
where it is the majority party but has to share the power in
the Cabinet with the Minseito ind it would like to take
advantage of the present dissatisfaction to overthrow the
Government. The snag lies in the uncertainty that the
Seiyukai would be called upon to form a party Government if
the Saito Cabinet should fall. They would never be satisfied

CODRSECOT
GOVERNMENT FINANCE
On November 25 final agreement was reached among
the various Ministries as to the budget estimates for
the coming fiscal year. The total amount of expenditur
on the general account for the fiscal year 1933-4 was
fixed at Yen 2,239,325,728.* It was estimated that it
would be necessary to issue loans to the amount of Yen
896,403,237 in order to meet these demands for expenditure.
If this be added to the present outstanding loans and the
loans in foreign currency be reduced to yen at the present
exchange rate, it will be seen that the total of the Japa
nese debt wi1l amount to ōver Yen 10,000,000,000.
The allotments of the Army and Navy under the above
projected buáget are Yen 447,883,252 and Yen 372,606,338,
respectively. As stated in last month's renort the mone-
tary demands of the Army and Navy caused consternation in
business circles of Japan and the result has been that
sectfons of the press which formerly feared to criticize,
even indirectly, any of the actions of the military have
now become bold enough to accuse the Army of endangering
Japan's economic stability und to demand that its appro-
priations be curtailed.*
There was a si mificant incident on the evening of
November 24 at a dinner of the Japan Chamber of Commerce
when Baron Gor, the President of the organization, and

FHRAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS
panese industry is now enjoying a period of
ospority which might be called exceptional ima world
where ecenomic conditions are generally so unsatisfacter
It is based on the depreciatlon of the yen and the en-
forced government spending and consequently there may he
day of reckoning which will be much worse than any that
preceded this period of activity. The cotton, yarn, and
rayon industries are going at full speed. The steel in-
dustry is profiting from army orders as well as the in-
dreased general industrial activity. The heavy industries
are buying raw materials in haste, partly because of the
increasing demand and partly because of the anticipation of
higher prices. The Railway Minister states that the index
number of industrial production in Japan (based on 1913 as
100) is 239, as compared with 84 in the United States, 75
in Great Britain, 93 in France, and 62 in Germany.
As for agriculture, the price of rice rose some in
November and the yen price of silk has more than doubled
since the fall in the yen. This has helped to solve the
problem of agrarian unrest that worried the Government so
much during the past summer.
The export of textiles has been booming in spite of
the boycott in China. New markets have been found in Africa
and the Near East.
The money market is lack and the country banks have
apparently been able to liquidate some of their frozen assets

ACTIVITIES OF AMERICAN COMMERC]
The strikerof the agentscand empio
Singer Sewing Machine Company.*
For several months the Singer Sewing Machl
Company has been faced with a very serious stri
agents and employees in Japan. A large part or the
business of this company is done on the installmont pi
The stocks of these machines as weil as the collectfons
the installments due from purchasers are in the handatof
their agents from whom the Company is unable to recover
them. The strikers refuse to return the sewing machihes
or the money collected to the Company. One recourse the
Company has is to sue their agents in the courts, which
not feasible because of the great number of the agents
and collectors. Accordingly the Company's representatives
desired to obtain cdministrative action by the police.
The police, however, are extremely reluctant to take action
in industrial disputes except in cases of violence
Violence has beei(tbly absent in this case, although
there have been many instances of intimidation.
While there has, so far, been nothing that would
w rrant diplomatic intervention, members of the Embassy
staff huve had some informal discussions with Foreign
Office officials, which have resulted in efforts by the
Home Department to settle the case.

MISCELLANEOUS:
Mamhuia
(a) Opium Monopoly in Manchuria*
It is disappointing to learn that a Japarese
Loan to the "Manchukuo" rogime is to be secured partly 
revenues accruing from the opium-monopoly. The Janamese
present a legalistic argument to justify themselves. They
state that the international engagements concerning opium
are not binding ipso jure upon the newly established
"Manchukuo"; that China has not ratified the Geneva Con-
vention of 1925 and that therefore only the Hague Con-
vention of 1912 is binding upon China; that a system of
Government monopoly of opium is in accord with the in.
tention of the Gonvention of 1912.
(b) Soviet Vessel with Scientists reaches Japan
via Northern Passage.
The Soviet ice-breaker SIBIRIAKOv, bearing a
scientific expedition hecded by Dr. Otto Schmidt, arrived
in Yokohama November 5, having come from Archangel via the
passage north of Siberia. According to press reports the
expedition which was sponsored by the Arctic Institute of
* Embassy's despatch No. 212 of December 2, 1932.

=== Page 378 === (confidence 0.98)
Leningrad, was for the threefold purpose of
whether the northeast passage from Hussle c
for commercial purposes, whether the rivars or Sther
could be entered from the west; and whether the yoyge
could be made in about two months. In his statement to
press reporters Dr. Schmidt stated that they leftArch
angel on July 28 and completed the passage to the Berin
Sea in the hoped-for two months. He stated that the out
standing event of the passage was the discovery, in the
Kara Sea east of Novaya Zemlya, of some islands which have
been unlnown to the world. He stated that it had been
found possible to enter the Lena River in northernmost
Siberia from the west. He also stated that although
the expedition reached 81 degrees north latitude it never
encountered any tremendous ice thickness.

Monthly Renort an Conditigna in Janán Du
the Month of Dagember, 19sn.
BUMMBY.
In its discussion of Japanese-American relations
and Japan's relations with the League-of Nations, this
report adds little to what has been reported in the press
and in telegrams from this Embassy. It describes the
rout of the "rebels" around Manchuli and the ominous signs
of approaching trouble near Shanhaikwan and the border of
Jehol. It summarizes the Japanese reaction to the Chinese-
Soviet resumption of relations, which has been fully
covered in another despatch of this Embassy. It sketehes
the developments in Japanese-Soviet relations and summarizes
the Japanese disarmament proposals.
In the realm of internal political affairs the Japanese
did little more than mark time during the month. This report
records some minor developments and rumors.
The prosperity reported in previous despatches continued
in Japan during the month of December though there were in-
creasing signs that it will be short-lived. A new exchange
control bill is being considered by the Government. December
marked the end of the period of preparation before the Banking
Law of 1927 became fully effective.
The lack of developments in the Singer Sewing Machine
dispute is commented on in this report.

POLITICAL RELATIONS WITH THE UNITED
Relations between Japan and the Unit
continued their even course during December. è
articles were noticed in the press which commented upo
the more friendly spirit shown by the United Statema
fact that the United States did not appear to be diotati
to the League of Nations produced a very favorable effopt
in Japan. One statement of the Japanese Ministry of War
referring to the Chinese situation read in part "some
time ago the Chinese politicians were working hard for
conflict between Japan and the United States and these two
people at that time seemed to entertain somewhat uneasy
feelings toward each other. But now all uneasiness is gone
and the Japanese and American peoples see no danger in the
way of their future relations." No comments have been made
by the press concerning the American investigations of the
"dumping" of Japanese goods in the United States though
officials of the Foreign Office have made inquiries of
members of the Embassy staff regarding the present status
of these investigations.
The Japanese Government has been considering sending
Count Kabayama and Viscount Ishii to the United States and
Europe on missions of goodwill.* No definite plans have
been announced yet. It is understood that the Japanese
delegation at Geneva opposed the proposal to send Viscount
Ishii to Europe before the end of the League sessions.
They felt that his visits to European capitals might em-
barrass them in their work in Geneva.
* Embassy's telegrams No. 278 f December 12-10 a.m. and
No. 284 of December 19-10 a.m.

POLITICAL RELATIONS WITA OEHER COUNS
(a) Japan and the Leegua of Matlon
December opened with the wyes af tha
interested in their country's foreign afreira still
concentrated on Geneva. Much to the disappointment or
the Counoil of the League had turned the Manohmrian mat
over to the Assembly for consideration by that bodyis fif
odd delegates. Japan felt that it had more to fear from
the smaller nations than from the Great Powers,"who ean
pass resolutions but would not assume any responstbility
in carrying them out." Mr. Matsuoka's speech of December
6
before the Assembly was widely acclaimed. The JAPAN
ADVERTISER termed it "the clearest statement of the Japa
nese case made by any Japanese official since the Man
churian outbreak". When the representatives of Czeoho-
slovakia, the Irish Free State, Sweden, and Spain sub-
mitted their resolution which was unfavorable to Japan
Mr. Matsuoka's demand for its withdrawal and his veiled
warning that its passage by the Assembly might mean the
withdrawal of Japan from the League were widely echoed
throughout Japan. The relief was evident when the final de-
cision was postponed by the Assembly's referring the re-
solution to the Committee of 19 without taking any action
itself.
When it was proposed in the Committee of 19 that a
Conciliation Commission be formed to include the United
States

=== Page 393 === (confidence 0.99)
States and Soviet Russia and the matter was ref
Japan for approval, the British Ambassador viaite
Japanese Foreign Minister and urged on behalf of his
Government that the Japanese Government give 1ta a
However, the Japanese Government took the stand that
League should look after its own affairs and not call
in outsiders.
The draft resolution prepared by a subcommittee
of the Committee of 19 was not accepted by the Japanese
Government, which announced that it would never accept
Chapters 9 & 10 of the Lytton Report nor any resolution
incompatible with its recognition of "Manchukuo" and
that it would insist that any commission of conciliation
which might be formed not participate in any negotiations
between China and Japan, but only prepare the way for such
negotiations. The last clause opens the way at least for
the establishment of some sort of commission. The ad-
journment of the Committee of 19 until after the New
Year postpones the verdict a little longer and this
is believed to be welcome to Japan.

credentials to the Chief Executive of the new regime on

According to naval experts tne plan would mean that

one battleship. The American tonnage in first class cruisera

destroyers, and submarines, Japan would be able to increase
her present equipment if her proposals were to become ef-
fective. What the Japanese military most fear in the event
of a war with a first closspower is the aeroplane. This
fear is evidenced by the proposal to abolish altogether
aircraft carriers and also landing platforms for naval
auroplanes on warships. The last provision would render
Japan immune from attack by air except from a base in
China or possibly Russia.

=== Page 404 === (confidence 0.99)
INTERNAL ECBNOMIC AND FINANOIAL CONDITIONS:
The prosperity reported in regent deapatehe
Embassy continued through the Month of December.
economists, and other observers have issued frequent
ings that it is a spurt and the opinion is gaining ground
in such circles that, barring unforeseen developments
a change for the worse will come about during the second
half of the current year. The Tokyo Stock Exchange has
been soaring to an extent reminiscent of the New York
Exchange early in 1929. Wholesale commodity prices rose 3.8%
during December and the index figures at the end of the year
were 25% above their level at the time of the imposition
of the gold embargo in December, 1932. Retail price index
figures have risen only 12% during the year.
The exchange control legislation passed at the last
session of the Diet is considered by the Government to be
inadequate for preventing speculation and it seems likely
that a new bill will be introduced at the coming session.
This will probably be broad enough to allow the Finance
Minister (1) to restrict importations to those accompanied
by evidence that exchange bills have been drawn; (2) to compel
the sale of foreign currency in the possession of Japanese
exchange banks; (3) to restrict future exchange transactions.
The Finance Minister a pears to be against pegging the yen
but he wants enough authority to take measures to prevent the
frequent fluctuations.
An Imperial Ordinance of December 28, 1932 raised the
rates for all cables sent from Japan, except press rates,
50%，

=== Page 405 === (confidence 0.97)
efrective January 1, 1033.
The month of December 193g marked the
period allowed by the Banking Law of 1927 for the
of Japan to make the major adjustments necessary for
operation of the law. After January 1, 1933 no banks or
than two million yen capitalization will be allowed in Toky
and Ogaka and no banks of less than one million yen capitai
zation will be allowed in the other cities of Japan. Smsil
banks may operate in the country districts. During the five
year period of grace it is estimated that nearly 1000 Japa-
nese banks have disappeared by merger, liquidation, or
change to other commercial activities. The eliminetion of
the smaller banks has been accomplished very smoothly and
there has been a marked desrease in bank failures during
recent years.

settled.
Both sides in the controversy have repeatedly been
informed that the Embassy is not in a position to act as
arbitrator in this purely private industrial dispute and
that the Japanese Government has machinery set up for this
purpose. The Company and the strikers have both been in-
formally advised by the Embassy to seek conciliation and to
settle the case through compromise if the business in Japan
1s not to be ruined. The Company nevertheless refuses to
make any concessions whatever and the strikers are equally
firm in their attitude.
The police contend that the Company should take the
matter to the courts. However, the cost of action against
each of the Company's agents who is holding the Company's
property illegally renders this action impractical. The
Company wishes the police to handle the case as an adminis-
trative one.

despatch No. 238 of December 30.

=== Page 412 === (confidence 0.99)
Despatch No. 278
Dated: February 2, 1933.
Subject: Monthly Political Report for January, 1933.
Summary:
In recounting the attack upon the Singer
Sewing Machine Company's offices in Yokohama, the
reports of American plots and schemes against Japan,
and the Japanese reaction to the passage of the Philip-
pine Independence bill this report summarizes the
contents of the telegr ms and reports forwarded during
the month to the Department and makes further minor
comments, especially on the second subject.
In the field of Japan's relations with other
countries January witnessed a gradual widening of the
breach betreen Japan and the Leaguc of Nations, the
apparontly successful efforts of the Japanese Cabinet
to "loc:lize" the Shanhaikwan affair, more obvious
preparations for an invasion of Jehol, and a worsen-
ing of Japan's relations vith Soviet Russia. This
monthly report discusses these cuestions, amplifying
other reports already made to the Department.
In spite of the widely accepted view that the
Saito Cabinet will resign upon the adjournment of the
Diet there are some indications that this may not
happen. The interpellations in the 64th Diet reveal
an undercurrent of opposition to the domination of
the Government by the military, aroused by the large
military

=== Page 413 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
military and naval budgets, but there seems to
be unanimous support of the Manchurian venture.
The important constitutional question as to what
authority determines the size of the army was
raised and answered in a hardly satisfactory
menner. Tokyo has been startled by the Govern-
ment's wholesale arrests of communists.
No important legislation has been passed by
the Diet though several bills have been introduced.
Tie Minister of Finance spoke in a very optimistic
vein when he introduced the budget bill and he has
been subjectd to severe questioning. The bill
will undoubtedly be passed but there appears to be
little confidence in his optimistic predictions
for a balanced budget in the future unless the
military are curbed. This is discussed wnder V.
GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
The general economic and financial conditions
of Japan remain the same. Business is enjoying
its spurt of prosperity apparently realizing that
it may not last.
The misccllaneous topics discussed in this
report are Japan's exhibit at Chicago's Century of
Progress, the resignation of the Mayor of Tokyo
because of corruption in his "official family",
the Opium loan to Manchuria, and the failure of
a government commission to decide whether Shrine
Shinto is a religion.

fices, and, before the police arrived, seriously injured
two Japanese and completely wrecked the interior of the
building. They demolished records, files, typewriters,
windows, and most of the furniture. When the police ar-
rived mostof the strikers were arrested.**

INTERNAL POLITICAL AFFAIRS
(a) The Cabinet.
The rumor, which was reported in last month's polit
cal report, that an agreement had been reached between the
Saito Cabinet and the Seiyukai providing that the formor
would reign upon the termination of the Diet session hes
since become more widespread. However, many well-informed
people believe that Premier Saito is becoming stronger
every day and may do something which will surprise the
political prognosticators. One danger to him is the at-
titude of the younger elements of the Seiyukai who may
decide to do something during the session which would
force the Premier's resignation. The Kokumin Domei, Mr.
Adachi's new party, would like to force a dissolution in
the hope of gaining more seats in the Diet in a general
election. They now have only 33 members of the Diet and
they believe that this does not represent their strength
in the country. The Minseito will support the Saito Govern-
ment and the wiser heads in the Seiyukai party do not want
a general election as they fear that their party would lose
some of its seats, possibly its majority. One hurdle which
the Seiyukai party sees ahead and which it will have to
overcome if it wishes to take over the government is the
attitude of the Genro. It is not at all certain that he
would advise the Emperor to call upon the Seiyukai to form
the Government in the case of the resignation of Premier
Saito, although it is understood that he does favor an
early return to strict party government. It is believed
in

=== Page 429 === (confidence 0.98)
uerters that the publierhas not ent
iss donfidence in'the political parties and a striet
party government might not gain the confidence af the
All of these factors may lead to Premier Salto
tion.
remaining in office in spite of the fact that thore is
widespread belief that he will resign as soon as the Diet
session ends and permit the installation of a Seiyukai
Government.*
At the time of the convening of the Diet after the
New Year holidays it was reported that a section of the
Seiyukai was going to force the Premier's resignation on
account of certain mistakes of etiquette which he had made
in the presence of His Majesty the Emperor. It was alleged
that at the opening of the Diet in December and again at
the New Year's Banquet on January 5, the Premier did certain
things which his enemies interpreted to mean lack of res-
pect for the Emperor. At one time it was reported that he
had offered his resignation but that the Emperor had refused
to accept it. Then it appeared that a section of the poli-
ticians was going to seize upon the incident to force him
out of office. However, the matter was finally dropped.
On January 9 Admiral Keisuke Okada submitted his resig-
nation as Japanese Minister of the Navy, and on the same
day Admiral Mineo Osumi was appointed by the Emperor to suc-
.ceed to the post. The resignation of Admiral Okada was
due to ill health and his reaching the age limit for active
service

(b) Opnosition to the domination of the Milital
The interpellations made during the present se
of the Diet reveal an undercurrent of opposition to the
domination of the military in Japanese politics. However,
the military identified themselves with the Manchurfan
venture, which has the endorsement of the mass of the
people, and the interpellators have been hard put to it
in attempting to oppose the military without opposing
the latter's main venture. The criticism of the military
is based upon the large sums demanded by them in the next
budget. No Japanese politician wishes to question the
need of financing the operations in Manchuria but there is
a suspicion that all the sums requested for increasing and
keeping up armaments are not necessary for the success of
the Manchurian affair.
The most talked-of interpellations on this matter were
those of Dr. H. Ashida, a member of the Lower House and
publisher of the JAPAN TIMES AND MAIL. Dr. Ashida was one
of the members of the Seiyukai selected by the party to
make interpellations. He asserted that Manchuria is now
being governed almost entirely by force by the Japanese
Army and he intimated that the Army now controls the foreign
policy of the country. He asked whether the Minister of
.War has the intention of forsaking the notion that the Army
is Almighty and why the Foreign Minister does not make up
his mind and carry out the diplomatic policy which he se-
lects as that which Japan should pursue.

 reply as to the authority which determines the millt

(c) Wholesale Arrests of Communists. **
The lifting of a press ban on January 18 revealed
to the starlted country a story of a wholesale roundup
of communists and "reds", in a series of raids. Lurid
newspaper accounts stated that many thousands of arrests
were made during November and December but it is belioved
that the number was only about 1,O00. The police claim
that they nipped in the bud a plot of the communists to
cause a mejor disturbance in Japan. The latter expected
Japan to be soon engaged in a foreign war and they were
planning to take advantage of the trouble to stage a re-
volution. The presence of a large number of young people,
sons and daughters of wealthy families, among those ar-
rested has caused much comment. Several professors and
two judiciary officers were also taken into custody.

The bill which has drawn most of the Diet's attenti
is the budget for the fisca1 year 1933-34. As statee els

regarding this bill and the sections covering the expendi-

However, it is believed that it will eventually be passed
without amendment.
The Department for Home Affairs has submitted a bill
for the revision of the Electoral Law. It provides for
transferring most of the expense of election campaigning
from the parties and candidates to the government. It also
provides for control by the prefectural government of the
electioneering, publishing of platforms, circulating campaign
literature among the voters, et cetera. There is understood
to be considerable opposition to this bill and it may not
become a law.
The Department of Agriculture and Forestry has submitted
a bill for agricultural insurance, another for the control of
the price of rice, and a third for the adjustment of agricul-
tural debts. The seoond and third bills call for very large
expenditures and the Ministry of Finance is said not to sup-
port them.

GOVERNMENT FINANCE.
Most of the interpellations during the present ses
ion of the Diet have been on the subject of the budget
for the fiscal year, beginning April 1, 19z3. Preyioua
monthly reports from this Embassy have given the principa1
features of the budget and it has been thoroughly disoussed
in other reports. Finance Minister Takahashi's speech in-
troducing it to the Diet was characterized by an optimism
that has been the subject of much criticism in the Diet
and the press. He predicted increasing revenue and de-
creasing expenditures for the future and a balanced budget
in a few years. He contended that revenues will increase
with the return of business to normalcy and that the large
expenditures for the Manchurian affair, improvement of ar-
maments, and rural relief are only temporary. In discuss-
ing the world depression he called attention to the "fortun-
ate" position of Japan in not being involved in the war
debts; nor being subject to pressure from foreign loans. He
attributed the fall in the value of the yen more to market
psychology than to the actual state of Japan's international
trade balance and emphasized the advantage gained thereby
in the field of foreign trade. He felt assured that the
Government could take care of its finances without causing
extreme inflation of the currency. The general tone of the
address of Mr. Takahashi is indicated by his summing up:
" To sum up, the monetary policy hitherto
pursued by the government, coupled with the
various emergency relief measures, has gradu-
ally given a better turn to our economic
world and is on the point of revivifying it.
I may say that I feel satisfied with this phe-
nomenon."
When

=== Page 435 === (confidence 0.97)
one eonwiders all tho stsns
om in Japan is largely temporary and all the war
gon,by her business leaders, it la not sirprimin
the speech of the Finance Minister was subjected to
criticism in the Diet, as well as in the press.
Soo
veices have been heard in the Diet favoring immedlate ink
creased taxation instead of loan issues to balance the pre-
ent budget, as proposed in the government's bill, but most
of the interpellations have dealt with the future. Many of
the Diet members apparently are not confident that the so
called "temporary expenditures" are properly termed. Many
feel that the temporary expenditures for armaments, for ins-
tanee, are very apt to become permanent if the military does
not show itself more amenable to suggestions of the civilian
part of the government in the future than it has in the
past. Many fear that the temporary costs of the Manchurian
affair may be repeated for many years to come if the present
policy is continued. Others think that the returning pros-
perity which the Minister relies upon to cause increasing
revenues in the future is not as certain as the Minister
seems to think. In other words, though the Diet members will
undoubtedly approve the budget for this year as proposed by
the government, they have clearly indicated that unanimity
does not exist in their hopes for the future and a section
has prepared the way for a future vigorous offensive against
the large military expenditures. Unless the military create
another "emergency" they may find it difficult to get all the
money they want in the future.

=== Page 436 === (confidence 0.98)

GÉNERAL ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL CONDITIONS.
There is little change to report in the general ee
D and finaneial condition of Japan during the month of Jar
The pri cipal industries of the country continue to onjoy
spurt of prosperity.
The Tokyo stock market registered a decline during the
month. Th1s was due partly to a natural reaction to the
bull market of previous months and partly to other causes,
such as the failure of the international situation to improve
the fear of higher tariffs for Japan's goods entering other
countries, the stability of the yen during recent months,
and the decision of the Bank of Japan to sell the new govern-
ment bond issues to the public instead of depositing them
in its vaults. The last two factors are indications to the
Japanese business man that extreme inflation of the currency
may not take place and therefore industrial profits may not
be as great in coming months as they have been during the
recent months of the declining yen.
Raw silk prices dropped during the month from Yen 1000
a bale to Yen 7o0, due to the falling off in the demand in
New York. The lowered price does not necessarily mean ruin
for the silk people as prices have recently been extremely
high. However, the drop is responsible for the reported
decision to keep the fil tories closed during the month of
February. They are closed every year during January because
of the cold weather but this year the period will be extend-
ed to two months.

(b) Mayor of Tokyo Resigns When his Aides are Ac-
cused of Bribery.
On January 18 the Mayor of Tokyo, Mr. Hidejiro Nagata,
resigned after a deputy mayor and another city official had
been formally indicted for having bribed the Tokyo Assembly
to secure Mr. Nagata's slection in 1930. The press stated
that Mr. Nagata is not accused of connivance but he resigned
after it was clear that the opposition to his election had
dissolved only after the two arrested officials had distri-
buted large sums of money among the Assemblymen who elected
him.

tioned in the Monthly Political Report for November

in Japan of the actual establishment of the opium monopoly
in "Manchukuo".
(d) Commission Unable to Decide Whether Shinto 1s
a religion.
A commission which has been working under the Home
04
Ministry for three years trying to decide whether Shrine
Shinto is a religion has reported that it has been unable
to reach a unanimous decision and will discontinue its
work for the time being. The Commission which was ap-
pointed in 1929 includes distinguished Shinto scholars and
high government officials. It was charged with submitting
a conclusion as to whether Shrine Shinto constitutes a re-
ligion. The subject is one to which a great deal of im-
portance attaches. For instance, the Ministry of Education
now forces students to go to certain shrines to pay their
respects to soldiers killed in battle, but if it should be
decided that Shrine Shinto is a religion the students could
refuse to attend these functions, since freedom of religious
belief is guaranteed by the Constitution. On the other hand
should Shrine Shinto not be considered a religion certain

=== Page 448 === (confidence 1.00)
Despatch No. 309.
Date: March 6, 1933.
Subject: Monthly Report on Conditions in Japan
during the month of February, 1933.
Summary:
The Japanese find little reason for hoping that
America will adopt a more lenient attitude toward them
with the advent of a Democratic administration. Many
fear that the United States will recognize Soviet
Russia and that the two will cooperate to a greater
extent in Far Eastern matters. However, some Japanese
feel that their country's withdrawal from the League
of Nations will offer her favorable opportunities
for drawing closer to the United States. The
National City Bank "spy" incident and the Singer
Sewing Machine Company's strike have been happily
settled.
The question of seceding from the League of
Nations was widely debated by the statesmen :nd the
press of Japan. The announcement on February 20
of the Cabinet's decision to withdraw if the Assembly
should adopt the report of the Committee of Nineteen
is evidence of the continued domintion of the military.
The Japanese appear not to expect an economic boycott
as they do not believe that the United States would take
part in one. Japan's delegate to the Disarmament Con-
ference is retaining his seat and the Japanese have every
intention of retaining their mandate over the former
German islands in the South Seas.
The advance into Jehol was started in the month
of February and appears to be succeeding with little
opposition

=== Page 449 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
opposition. It is believed that the War Office sincerely
desires to confine the operations of the Army to the
territory north of the Great Wall but they are appre-
hensive lest the commanders in the field get out of
hand and advance towards Peiping.
Japanese relations with Soviet Russia during the
month were not marked by developments other than the
departure of the Soviet Ambassador and a "spy" incident
of little significance.
The internal political situation continues uncertain.
It is not known what will happen after the adjournment of
the Diet. The murderers of ir. Inouye nd Baron Dan have
been inaicted. The Young Officers' League has been active
again. Mr. Ozaki, the liberal politician, has returned
to J pan with much publicity. Mr. Ikki, the liberal
Minister of the Imperial Household, has resigned.
The Diet sessions have been dull and, except for the
budget for 1935-34 which has passed the Lower House, no
important legislation has been passed.
With the passage of certain budgetary measures it is
possible to state that the cost of the Manchurian venture
has been about 563 million yen. The South Manchuria
Railway has announced its intention to double its capital.
The outstanding development in the Japanese business
world during the month was the collapse of the Tokyo
stock market.
The new budget carries an item to finance emigration to
Brazil. The Overseas Minister has invited the attention of
the Diet to the problem of educating the Japanese who have
settled in other countries.

Makino, and Baron Shidehara worked hard to prevent the
Cabinet from taking a decision in favor of secessiom from
the League. However, the Foreign Minister and the War and
Navy Ministers won their point.
The Army, through the ex-servicemen's organization.
has led the fight. Mass meetings have been hold in all
parts of the country, denouncing the League of Nations
and avocating immediate resignation by Japan. Two big
meetings were held in Tokyo on February 22 which were
attended by members of all political parties. Some of
the organizers of the agitation in favor of secession
undoubtedly hoped that it would influence the League to
come to a decision favorable to Japan. However, others
sincerely desire Japan to cut herself off politically
fromthe rest of the world and still others have advocated
secession because they believe that remaining in the
League after the adoption of the unfavorable report would
not be consistent with national dignity.

The secession of Japan from the League of Nations
raises two other related questions- possible withdrawal
from the Disarmament Conference and the status of the
Japanese Mandates. The Army has been strongly advocating
the withdrawal of the Japanese delegation from the Dis-
armament Conference. However, the Department of Foreign
Affaird has advocated continuation with that Conference
and it appears to have won its point for the present.
Japan's delegate has remained at the Conference.

Japanese claim to retention of the mandated islenda
as follows:
1. Germany ceded her overseas possessions to the Alls
and the Allies, not the League, allotted the mandates
under Article 22 of the Versailles Treaty and not under
the Covenant of the League of Nations.
2. The mandates are not necessarily limited to men
of the League( witness the request to the United States to
assume the mandate over Armenia) and therefore should
Japan cease to be a member of the League, this fact would
not automatically cancel the mandate.
3. The division of the former German territories
among the Allies had been fixed by secret treaties in 1917
but annexation was abandoned because of the objections of
Mr. Woodrow Wilson. The system of mandates was adopted
to camouflage what amounted to annexation.

On February 2, fourteen members of the so-called Blood
Brotherhood League were indicted. Thirteen were charged
with murder and one for aiding and abetting murder. They
were all arrested within a few weeks after the assassination
of Baron Dan and are charged with murdering not only him
but also Mr. Junnosuke Inouye. Their trial will probably
take place in the fall.
The Young Officers League became active again during
February. On February 19th they issued a manifesto urging
withdrawal from ths League. They atacked Baron Shidehara,
Count Makino, and Admiral Suzuki, Lord Chamberlain for op-
posing secession from the League. On the next day twenty-
seven representatives of the Kokka Shakaito(National Socialist
Party) called at Baron Shidehara's house. Although they did
not see him they read a resolution calling upon him to take
a strong attitude and to lead public opinion faithfully.
These demonstrations were caused by a visit which Baron

Mr. Tukio Ozaki, the famous liberal politiciam
and former Mayor of Tokyo, who has been abroad for
months, has now returned to Japan. He has stated that
he returns to study conditions here. His liberal state-
ments made while abroad have drawn the fire of the militars
and the reactionaries and the police were forced to take
at Kobe, Osaka and Yokohama. Mr. Ozaki has openly said
that he may be assassinated while in Japan but that he will
give up his life rather than refrain from saying what he
thinks. Many newspapers have expressed admiration for
his spirit though disagreeing with his views on the present
situati@n. A certain amount of toleration will probably
be shown toward him for the time being because of his
age, prestige and the fact that he is bringing to Japan
the ashes of his wife who died in England.
Dr. Kitokuro Ikki, Minister of the Imperial Household,
resigned on February 15 and was replaced by Mr. Kurahei
Yuasa, President of the Board of Audits. Dr. Ikki is said
to have wanted to resign several months ago but was persuaded
to remain. He has been opposed by the military because
of his liberal views. Dr. Yuasa was Chief of the

=== Page 467 === (confidence 0.99)
COURSE OF LEGIBLATION
The Diet was in regular session duriau
menth but its proeeedings were marked neithar,by
accomplishment of constructive legislation nor by
the bitter debates and "fireworks" which have.some
times in the past drawn to it the attention, as well
as the disgust, of the public. It has passed seteral
supplementary budget bills , which would be called
Deficiency Bills in the United States; and other minor
financial measures. The budget for 1933-4 was passed
by the lower house but had not passed the House of
Peers when the month ended. Its main provisions have
not been changed from what they were when discussed in
previous monthly reports of this Embassy. The Exchange
Control Law has also been passed by the Lower House.
It gives wide powers to the Minister of Finance to
control exchange transactions.
Discussions in the Diet have been very mild. The
members apnar not to have dared to criticize the appro-
priations for the Army and Navy and have gone no farther
than to express their disbelief in the Finance Minister's
theory that government expenditures will decline in future
years.
An appropriation in the budget for completing the
construction of a harbor at Rashin, Korea, was subjected
to some criticism on the grounds that that port was said
to be ice-bound for several months of the year. The
Minister

=== Page 470 === (confidence 0.99)
The outstanding event in Japanese busts
during Febrpary was the collapse of the Tokyo sto
which began on the 14th. Before the end of the month
the value of the securities listed had declined by abo
a half billion yen. The collapse is said to have bes
due, first, to the inflation of values which has.takn
place since the imposition of the gold embargo; secondly
to the announced intention of Japan to secede from the
League of Nations; and, thirdly, to the increased obsts of
production in industry. The enormous profits of Japa-
nese industries during recent months were due largely
to the fall in the exchange but now that the raw materials
bought from abroad when the yen was at par have been used
up it is not possible to make the profits which were formerly
made.
The wholesale price index in Tokyo dropped 2.9 per cent
during the month of February. This was due to a drop in
foodstuffs and in all textiles.
The accrued unfavorable trade balance since the
beginning of the culendar year was, on March 1, Yen
126,647,000.
There are indications that an iron merger will be
successfully uccomplished in Japan in the near future.
The steel plants owned by the Government will be merged with
the privately owned plants, the Government owning a control-
ling proportion of the stock and supervising the operation
of the new concern.

ABOR AND EMTGRATION:
The budget for lass-4 carries an item of
Ien 5, 744,749 for the protect1on and encgura
emigrants and oversea exploitation and colonissti
prises. It is understood that most of the above:aum
be used on projects for emigration to Brazil. Thbre
a separate item of Yen 382,075 for emigration1
In reply to an interpellation in the Diet the Mi
of Overseas Affairs invited the attention of the
to the problem of educating the Japanese who live over
He stressed the economic difficulties now being enogunt
by the Japanese who have settled in Brazil. He stated the
the overseas colonies of Japanese subjects are generally
provided with primary schools but they must return to
Japan to secure higher education. He referred to the oft-
expressed desire of the Japanese Government that its
subjects who have settled abroad should be encouraged to
be citizens of the countries in which they have settled,
but he called attention to the difficulties of their
assimilation with the local populations. His con-
clusion was that some provision should be made to instill
into these colonists some elements of the Japanese manner
of thinking and to furnish them with institutions of higher
education.
